2027 Medicare Advantage and Part D Star Ratings Note: The information included in this Fact Sheet is based on the 2027 Medicare Advantage (MA) and Part D Star Ratings published on the Medicare Plan Finder on Oct. 8, 2026. For details on the methodology of the MA and Part D Star Ratings, please refer to the 2027 Part C & D Star Ratings Technical Notes available at http://go.cms.gov/partcanddstarratings . Ensuring that Medicare works for seniors and people with disabilities and that people with Medicare have access to robust, stable, high-quality, and affordable options for the coverage they need are top priorities for the Centers for Medicare & Medicaid Services (CMS). CMS continues to implement enhancements to the Medicare Advantage (MA) and Medicare Part D Star Ratings programs to incentivize plans to strive for ongoing quality improvement and to help ensure that Medicare enrollees receive high-quality care. CMS publishes the MA (Medicare Part C) and Part D Star Ratings each year to measure the quality of health and prescription drug services received by consumers enrolled in MA and Part D prescription drug plans (PDPs or Part D plans). An important component of the Star Ratings is to provide people with Medicare and their caregivers with meaningful information about the quality of plans in Medicare Plan Finder, alongside information about benefits and costs, to assist them in comparing Medicare health and drug plans and choosing the Medicare coverage option that best fits their health needs. The 2027 Star Ratings announced today appear on Medicare Plan Finder for 2027 Open Enrollment, which begins on October 15, 2026 and runs through December 7, 2026, and will impact 2028 MA quality bonus payments. Highlights of Contract Performance in 2027 Star Ratings MA Prescription Drug (MA-PD) contracts are rated on up to 43 unique quality and performance measures; MA-only contracts (without Part D coverage) are rated on up to 32 measures; and standalone PDP contracts are rated on up to 13 measures. For each measure, CMS establishes thresholds called “cut points” that are used to determine whether each contract’s performance for that measure receives a one, two, three, four, or five-star rating. Changes in the Methodology for the 2027 Star Ratings CMS made changes to the methodology for the 2027 Star Ratings through previous notice and comment rulemaking. There are two new measures added beginning with the 2027 Star Ratings: Concurrent Use of Opioids and Benzodiazepines (COB) and Polypharmacy: Use of Multiple Anticholinergic Medications in Older Adults (Poly-ACH). i The Care for Older Adults — Functional Status Assessment measure is returning to the 2027 Star Ratings after substantive specification changes. i The respecified Colorectal Cancer Screening measure was moved into the 2027 Star Ratings as a new measure, replacing the legacy Colorectal Cancer Screening measure. i All of these measures are process measures developed from evidence-based clinical treatment guidelines, each with a weight of one. These clinical process measures are critical for measuring healthcare and medication use quality, ensuring that care is delivered effectively, and preventing long-term health consequences by addressing potential health concerns early in the beneficiary’s care. The weight of the Improving or Maintaining Physical Health and Improving or Maintaining Mental Health measures increased from a weight of one for the 2026 Star Ratings to a weight of three beginning with the 2027 Star Ratings following a substantive measure specification change. The Medication Therapy Management (MTM) Program Completion Rate for Comprehensive Medication Review (Part D) measure i was transitioned to the display page for measurement years 2025 and 2026 and will return to the Star Ratings as a new measure beginning with the 2029 Star Ratings (measurement year 2027). Additionally, the Care for Older Adults — Pain Assessment (Part C) ii and Medication Reconciliation Post-Discharge (Part C) i measures were retired from the Star Ratings. For the 2027 Star Ratings, CMS began using data collected through the Part C Reporting Requirements (as described at § 422.516(a)) to confirm the completeness of the Independent Review Entity (IRE) data used in the calculation of the Plan Makes Timely Decisions about Appeals and Reviewing Appeals Decisions measures. i As codified at § 422.164(g)(1)(iii), CMS used these data to implement scaled reductions data integrity issues were identified. Rating Distribution iii The last row in Table 1 shows the trend in the average overall Star Ratings weighted by enrollment for MA-PDs from 2024-2027 after any adjustments for extreme and uncontrollable circumstances. iv Approximately 37% of MA-PDs (188 contracts) that will be offered in 2027 earned four stars or higher for their 2027 overall rating. Weighted by enrollment, approximately 71% of MA-PD enrollees are currently in contracts that will have four or more stars in 2027. Changes in Star Ratings year-over-year are normal and expected and vary by measure. Star Ratings are intended to capture a contract’s performance during the measurement period, with some contracts performing better or worse in different years. The cut points are recalculated each year based on performance during the measurement period. The 2026 overall rating data reflect the updates for MA contracts that had their 2027 Quality Bonus Payment rating updated in June 2026 and their 2026 overall rating updated in Medicare Plan Finder in July 2026. ### [Conversational diagnostic artificial intelligence in ambulatory primary care: a prospective feasibility study.](https://doi.org/10.1016/s0140-6736(26)01535-7)

- Source: `lancet_abstracts` · published `2026-10-08` · freshness `stale`
- Reading value: `pending` · status: `ineligible`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `pmid:42849491`

<h4>Background</h4>Artificial intelligence (AI)-based systems show promise for assisting primary care providers (PCPs) with patient care. We aimed to evaluate the safety and quality of clinical conversations of a patient-facing conversational AI system, which engaged in real-world urgent primary care appointments.<h4>Methods</h4>In this prospective, single-centre, single-arm feasibility study, English-speaking patients aged at least 18 years interacted with the Articulate Medical Intelligence Explorer (AMIE) up to 5 days before a single-complaint urgent primary care appointment. Physician safety supervisors monitored all interactions and were trained to intervene on the basis of predefined safety criteria. AMIE transcripts and summaries were shared with PCPs before the visit. Primary outcomes were the number of supervised conversation safety stops, AMIE's conversation quality assessed by clinical evaluators, and patient and PCP experiences per surveys. This study is registered with ClinicalTrials.gov (NCT06911398).<h4>Findings</h4>From April to November, 2025, 114 patients were enrolled with 98 completing both the AMIE interaction and the PCP appointment. Zero conversation safety stops were required on the basis of prespecified criteria. Safety supervisors noted one hallucination and added clinical information in five interactions. AMIE's conversations were rated favourably in 87-100% of cases (17 criteria) by clinical evaluators, and 48-96% (16 criteria) by patients. Patient attitudes towards AI improved after interacting with AMIE and remained elevated after the patient's visit with their physician. PCPs completed post-surveys in 60 of 98 cases, including 44 cases in which they reviewed the AMIE transcript before the visit. PCPs found AMIE helpful for visit preparation in 33 of 44 cases and reported that it might have changed their behaviour in 25 of 44 cases.<h4>Interpretation</h4>Although further research is needed, this study shows the initial feasibility of conversational AI in a real-world setting-assessed via conversation safety and quality, as well as user acceptance-and represents a crucial step towards clinical translation.<h4>Funding</h4>Alphabet.

### [Nicotinamide Riboside for Patients With Early-Stage Parkinson Disease: The NOPARK Randomized Clinical Trial.](https://doi.org/10.1001/jama.2026.18828)

- Source: `jama_abstracts` · published `2026-10-08` · freshness `stale`
- Reading value: `pending` · status: `ineligible`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `pmid:42848371`

<h4>Importance</h4>Nicotinamide riboside, a nicotinamide adenine dinucleotide precursor, has been proposed as a potential disease-modifying therapy for Parkinson disease based on preclinical and early studies. Whether prolonged oral nicotinamide riboside improves clinical outcomes in Parkinson disease is unknown.<h4>Objective</h4>To determine whether nicotinamide riboside improves clinical outcomes in Parkinson disease compared with placebo.<h4>Design, setting, and participants</h4>Randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled, phase 3 clinical trial conducted at 11 centers in Norway. Eligible participants were patients aged 35 years or older with Parkinson disease diagnosed within 2 years before enrollment, Hoehn and Yahr disease stage less than 3, abnormal dopamine transporter scintigraphy findings confirming dopaminergic nigrostriatal denervation, and stable dopaminergic treatment before enrollment. Of 537 screened individuals, 410 were randomized from May 6, 2020, to June 20, 2024, and 393 were included in the primary analysis. Final follow-up was completed on June 19, 2025.<h4>Interventions</h4>Patients were randomized to receive oral nicotinamide riboside, 500 mg twice daily (n = 206), or matching placebo (n = 204) for 52 weeks.<h4>Main outcomes and measures</h4>The primary outcome was change from baseline to week 52 in the Movement Disorder Society Unified Parkinson Disease Rating Scale total score (MDS-UPDRS parts I-III; range, 0-236, with higher scores indicating more severe disease; clinically important differences for improvement and worsening, 6.7 and 5.2 points, respectively), analyzed using a mixed model for repeated measures. The 5 key secondary outcomes were the individual parts I, II, and III of the MDS-UPDRS; dopamine transporter single-photon emission computed tomography brain imaging findings; and the Non-Motor Symptoms Scale score (NMSS; range, 0-360, with higher scores indicating greater nonmotor symptom burden).<h4>Results</h4>Among the 393 participants in the primary analysis, the mean age was 65.6 (SD, 9.4) years and 133 (34%) were female. At week 52, the total MDS-UPDRS score increased by 2.45 (95% CI, 0.80-4.10) points with nicotinamide riboside and decreased by 0.27 (95% CI, -1.95 to 1.40) points with placebo, yielding an adjusted mean difference of 2.72 points (95% CI, 0.47-4.98 points; P = .02), favoring placebo. Of the 5 prespecified key secondary outcomes, 4 showed no significant difference, but nonmotor symptom burden worsened more with nicotinamide riboside (NMSS score adjusted mean difference, 4.22 [95% CI, 1.07-7.37] points; P = .009). Serious adverse events occurred in 17 (8.3%) of 205 participants receiving nicotinamide riboside and 29 (14.1%) of 205 receiving placebo in the safety population.<h4>Conclusions and relevance</h4>Among patients with early Parkinson disease, nicotinamide riboside treatment for 52 weeks did not improve clinical outcomes and was associated with worse clinical outcomes than placebo. These findings do not support nicotinamide riboside as a disease-modifying treatment for Parkinson disease.<h4>Trial registration</h4>ClinicalTrials.gov Identifier: NCT03568968.

### [Oral deucrictibant for on-demand treatment of hereditary angioedema attacks: a phase 3, multicentre, randomised, double-blind, placebo-controlled crossover trial.](https://doi.org/10.1016/s0140-6736(26)01296-1)

- Source: `lancet_abstracts` · published `2026-10-08` · freshness `stale`
- Reading value: `pending` · status: `ineligible`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `pmid:42849489`

<h4>Background</h4>In hereditary angioedema, a rare bradykinin-mediated condition, oral on-demand treatments facilitating prompt administration and providing rapid, sustained relief and attack resolution remain scant. We investigated the efficacy and safety of deucrictibant, an oral bradykinin B2 receptor antagonist, for on-demand treatment of hereditary angioedema attacks.<h4>Methods</h4>RAPIDe-3 was a phase 3, double-blind, 2 × 2 crossover trial in adolescent and adult participants (aged ≥12 to <18 years and aged ≥18 to ≤75 years, respectively) with hereditary angioedema, at 59 sites across 24 countries on six continents. Eligible participants had hereditary angioedema, including those with normal C1 inhibitor associated with a documented genetic variant, at least two attacks within the last 3 months before screening, and experience with effectively using conventional on-demand therapies for hereditary angioedema attacks. Participants were randomly allocated in a 1:1 ratio to one of two double-blind crossover sequences to treat two attacks with a 20 mg oral deucrictibant immediate-release capsule or placebo as either deucrictibant-placebo or placebo-deucrictibant, stratified by age and use of long-term prophylaxis. The primary endpoint was time to onset of symptom relief, defined as Patient Global Impression of Change rating of at least a little better. Safety was assessed in all participants who received any dose of study drug. RAPIDe-3 is registered with ClinicalTrials.gov (NCT06343779) and is complete.<h4>Findings</h4>Between April 4, 2024, and Sept 1, 2025, 149 people were assessed for eligibility, and 134 eligible participants (ten [7%] adolescents and 124 [93%] adults) were randomly allocated. 58 (43%) were male and 76 (57%) were female, and the median age was 38·0 years (IQR 27·0-50·0). 113 participants treated 201 attacks. The primary efficacy analysis included 88 participants with paired treated attacks. Of these, 47 (53%) participants were female and 41 (47%) were male, and 60 (68%), 16 (18%), 4 (5%), and 1 (1%) were White, Asian, Black, and American Indian or Alaska Native, respectively. Onset of symptom relief occurred significantly faster with deucrictibant than placebo, with median times of 1·28 h (95% CI 1·05 to 1·52) and more than 12 h (5·82 to >12), respectively (p<0·0001). Fatigue was the only adverse event reported more than once within 3 days post-treatment: a single event was reported by two participants, one of which was deemed unrelated to treatment. Across the study, no treatment-related adverse events were assessed as severe or serious, and no adverse events led to treatment discontinuation.<h4>Interpretation</h4>Oral deucrictibant provided faster times to symptom relief than placebo and was generally well tolerated. These results support the use of deucrictibant as potentially the first orally administered bradykinin B2 receptor antagonist for the treatment of attacks of bradykinin-mediated angioedema, including hereditary angioedema, and may address an unmet need for options that are effective and well tolerated, with convenient administration.<h4>Funding</h4>Pharvaris.

### [Exploring College Students’ Compliance During a 60-Day Alcohol-Related Ecological Momentary Assessment Using Location- and Time-Based Questions: Prospective Observational Study](https://mhealth.jmir.org/2026/1/e81713)

<strong>Background:</strong> Ecological momentary assessment (EMA) methods can provide assessment of alcohol-related beliefs and behaviors when people are in their natural environments. An increasingly common EMA approach involves the use of passive sensors (eg, continuous sharing of Bluetooth and GPS cell phone data) to collect rich data and trigger alcohol assessment in specific contexts. While collecting real-time assessments allows researchers to explore contextual factors impacting alcohol use, there is a lack of research examining compliance rates to alcohol-related EMAs that use daily questions for study periods over 2 weeks. EMAs over longer periods allow researchers to assess meaningful changes over time and sample low-base-rate events. Further, there is limited research exploring how EMA compliance is influenced by individual-level factors (eg, privacy concerns and alcohol use). Identifying factors associated with compliance can help inform EMA protocol design. <strong>Objective:</strong> This study had 2 primary goals. First, we add to the literature by examining the feasibility and acceptability of a 60-day college student alcohol-EMA that uses phone-based, passive sensors. Second, we examined whether compliance was associated with event-level alcohol use, privacy concerns, and types of questions asked (ie, questions triggered by time or GPS location). <strong>Methods:</strong> College students (N=68) who reported regular alcohol use completed the 60-day EMA study. At baseline, participants completed an online survey that included an assessment of privacy concerns. During the study period, participants completed up to 15 questions a day, including evening and morning self-reports of alcohol use. Feasibility was assessed based on enrollment, retention, and compliance rates. Acceptability was examined at the end of the study period using open- and close-answered questions on perceptions of the EMA experience. <strong>Results:</strong> The findings supported the feasibility of the 60-day EMA with enrollment exceeding benchmarks and high compliance across the study period (average compliance=90%). Overall, students found the protocol acceptable with 88% (58/66) indicating that they would be willing to complete another EMA study. Participants’ compliance was not associated with alcohol use at the event-level (odds ratio [OR] 0.94, 95% CI 0.80-1.10) or at the trait level (OR 0.92, 95% CI 0.73-1.17). Participants’ compliance was greater for time-trigger assessment than GPS-triggered questions (<i>P</i>&lt;.001). Privacy concerns were not associated with reduced responding. <strong>Conclusions:</strong> This study has implications for those designing alcohol EMAs for college populations that involve the use of passive sensors, including recommendations for balancing predictable timing of questions and variability in question content.

### [How People Integrate Mental Health Apps Into Everyday Self-Care: Qualitative Study](https://mhealth.jmir.org/2026/1/e95414)

- Source: `jmir_mhealth` · published `2026-10-09` · freshness `stale`
- Reading value: `pending` · status: `ineligible`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `url:https://mhealth.jmir.org/2026/1/e95414`

Background: Mobile mental health (MH) apps are increasingly promoted as tools for self-care. However, less is known about how users integrate them into everyday life and how they combine with other forms of support. Objective: This qualitative study examined what motivates individuals to adopt MH apps and how these tools are used alongside other care practices. Methods: We conducted semistructured interviews with 31 Czech MH app users aged 18‐56 (23 women), recruited through social media, researchers’ networks, and snowball sampling. Eligible participants had used an MH app for at least 2 weeks; individuals currently experiencing a psychological crisis were excluded. Interviews were conducted online or face-to-face and analyzed using reflexive thematic analysis by Braun and Clarke. The analysis was inductive and informed by a critical realist epistemological stance. Initial analytic engagement was conducted independently, followed by collaborative theme development and refinement. Results: We identified the following three motivational patterns for MH app use: (1) regular use to maintain well-being and support preventive self-care; (2) reactive use during periods of acute distress; and (3) temporary, problem-focused use aimed at understanding or addressing a specific difficulty. Beyond motivations for adoption, our findings revealed the following four distinct trajectories of app engagement and disengagement: (1) emancipation, with apps acting as ongoing support for self-care when therapy is reduced or ended; (2) independence, as techniques learned through apps are transferred into everyday life and used without the app; (3) reliance, where users persist with app-based self-care despite insufficient benefit, occasionally delaying or limiting other forms of help; and (4) episodic use, characterized by intermittent engagement during specific life periods. These findings extend existing research by showing that disengagement is not necessarily a marker of failure but can reflect successful self-care and the transfer of learned skills. We also identified tensions and unintended consequences, including the burden of sustaining engagement, risks of overreliance, emotional costs associated with sustained self-monitoring, and tensions between digital self-care and efforts to reduce screen use. Participants generally viewed self-monitoring as beneficial when guided by clear personal goals and embedded within a broader system of self-care. Conclusions: Our findings suggest that appropriate disengagement can signal a successful step in self-care. App design and policy should acknowledge episodic use and strengthen connections to human support with clear guidance and accessible pathways to additional care when needed.

### [Addressing threats to future global health-what is the right timeframe?](https://doi.org/10.1016/s0140-6736(26)00880-9)

- Source: `lancet_abstracts` · published `2026-10-10` · freshness `stale`
- Reading value: `pending` · status: `ineligible`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `pmid:42858756`

Our decisions today can support or threaten future human health. Insofar as we protect the planet or invest in fruitful research on pandemic countermeasures, we promote future wellbeing and health, sometimes at the present generation's expense. As we warm the planet, accelerate antibiotic resistance, or consume finite natural resources, we might serve present-day wellbeing or health at the expense of future generations. To decide which such trade-offs are legitimate or advisable, we need to know how to balance the health interests of future generations against each other and against our own. Should concerns for future health be confined to the 21st century as per the stated mission of the Lancet Commission on 21st century global threats to health? If not, how far into the future should they extend, and why? Population ethics is a branch of ethics that considers future populations' wellbeing. This Review, a part of the Lancet Commission on 21st century global threats to health, applies general insights from population ethics to future populations' health, and shows why that application is more complex than meets the eye.

### [Strengthening health systems to prioritise, provide care, and prevent osteoarthritis: a roadmap for global health policy.](https://doi.org/10.1016/s0140-6736(26)01700-9)

- Source: `lancet_abstracts` · published `2026-10-11` · freshness `stale`
- Reading value: `pending` · status: `ineligible`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `pmid:42860133`

By 2050, almost 1 billion people are expected to live with osteoarthritis-a disabling musculoskeletal condition with currently no cure. Despite its burden and costs, osteoarthritis remains largely invisible in national and global health agendas, is poorly integrated within service models for other chronic health conditions, and is frequently perceived as inevitable with ageing and associated with limited therapeutic options. This context drives an osteoarthritis identity problem that fuels frustration among those living with the disease and health-care professionals, perpetuates stigma and inequities, undermines access to effective interventions, and crucially, deprioritises osteoarthritis in health policy. How can health systems be strengthened to mitigate the burden of disease and prioritise the prevention and control of osteoarthritis? We outline a co-created, globally relevant roadmap for health policy responses for osteoarthritis prevention and care that extend beyond the UN's Agenda 2030 targets. The intended audience for the roadmap includes health policy makers, health services managers, clinicians, and civil society organisations. The roadmap anchors to three strategic priorities underpinned by guiding principles of a life course approach, prioritising health equity, and ensuring that responses are affordable and sustainable across economies. Priority 1: recognise and respond to osteoarthritis with capacity building in national surveillance of burden, cost, and modifiable risk factors, and explicitly embedding musculoskeletal health within national and subnational health policies. Priority 2: scale up high-value, person-centred, equitable care for approximately 600 million people living with osteoarthritis. This priority requires reframing of societal narratives around osteoarthritis and reconfiguring service models to integrate osteoarthritis into primary and chronic disease care while also addressing social, environmental, and commercial determinants of musculoskeletal health. Priority 3: slow the osteoarthritis trajectory via primary and secondary prevention targeted at excess bodyweight and joint injury, enabled by cross-sectoral and cross-disease cooperation. These priorities are supported by accelerators requiring global collaboration, within and outside the osteoarthritis community.

### [Development and Validation of an Adult Medical Urgency Score for US Donor Heart Allocation.](https://doi.org/10.1001/jama.2026.17403)

- Reading value: `pending` · status: `ineligible`

<h4>Importance</h4>One in 5 heart transplant candidates in the US die or are delisted without transplant. Donor hearts are currently allocated by a 6-status therapy-based system criticized for limited risk stratification and susceptibility to gaming.<h4>Objective</h4>To develop a new heart allocation policy based on an objective medical urgency score that ranks candidates based on the interaction between physiology and mechanical circulatory support.<h4>Design, setting, and participants</h4>Registry-based observational study using data from the US organ transplant system. All adult (aged ≥18 years) heart transplant candidates from January 2019 to August 2025 were included, split into training (2019-2023), validation (2023-2024), and test (2024-2025) datasets.<h4>Main outcomes and measures</h4>A marginal structural model of 6-week mortality without transplant was estimated (the US Candidate Risk Score [US-CRS]). Eight models were compared in held-out data by weighted time-dependent area under the curve (AUC) and calibration by status. Finally, US-CRS was converted into an allocation policy and a match-run simulation of waitlist reordering was performed compared with the current 6-status system.<h4>Results</h4>Adult heart transplant candidates were partitioned into consecutive training (n = 21 859), validation (n = 7897), and test (n = 8256) datasets (mean [SD] age, 53 [13] years; 74% male). The chosen US-CRS model included 5 laboratory values and 4 therapeutic devices (durable left ventricular assist device, intra-aortic balloon pump, microaxial flow pump, and advanced mechanical circulatory support). In test-set cross-sections of candidates actively waiting on the same calendar date, US-CRS had similar or significantly higher time-dependent AUC than the current 6-status system (mean AUC, 0.807 vs 0.693, respectively) and acceptable calibration by status, especially for candidates who received their status by exception. In match-run analysis (n = 45 530 donor hearts), a US-CRS-based system led to 67% of transplant recipients moving down in rank order and increased observed 6-week transplant-censored mortality for the first candidate on the match run from 11.2% (95% CI, 10.1%-12.5%) under the current system to 15.7% (95% CI, 13.9%-17.6%) under the US-CRS-based system.<h4>Conclusions and relevance</h4>In this registry-based study of US heart transplant candidates, a US-CRS-based allocation system prioritized sicker candidates with higher mortality without transplant compared with the current medical urgency statuses.

### [Heart Transplant for Advanced Heart Failure: A Review.](https://doi.org/10.1001/jama.2026.17622)

- Source: `jama_abstracts` · published `2026-10-12` · freshness `stale`
- Reading value: `pending` · status: `ineligible`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `pmid:42862549`

<h4>Importance</h4>Heart failure affects more than 6 million US adults; approximately 4% to 5% progress to advanced disease despite optimal therapy. Heart transplant is a class I guideline-recommended therapy for select individuals with advanced heart failure.<h4>Observations</h4>Early referral for heart transplant is guided by the "I NEED HELP" mnemonic, a tool that identifies patients with advanced heart failure based on criteria such as use of inotropes, persistent symptoms (New York Heart Association class III or IV), end-organ dysfunction, left ventricular ejection fraction, defibrillator shocks, hospitalizations for heart failure, persistent edema or escalating use of diuretics, and low blood pressure. Each criterion is associated with higher risk of death or first heart failure hospitalization over a median follow-up of 260 days (adjusted hazard ratio, 1.19 [95% CI, 1.11-1.27] for each additional criterion). Typical absolute contraindications to heart transplant include active systemic infection, malignancy with limited life expectancy, severe and irreversible end-organ dysfunction (eg, cirrhosis, severe pulmonary hypertension), active substance use, major untreated psychiatric illness, inadequate social support, and severe frailty. Approximately 4600 heart transplants are performed annually in the US. The mortality rate was 8.5 deaths/100 patient-years for adults on the transplant waiting list in 2023. Leading causes of early death after transplant include primary graft dysfunction (39.5%), multiorgan failure (18.5%), and infection (13.9%). Among adults, contemporary survival rates after heart transplant are 91.5% at 1 year and 80.3% at 5 years. Lifelong maintenance immunosuppression typically combines a calcineurin inhibitor, antiproliferative agents (typically mycophenolate mofetil or azathioprine) and corticosteroids, with selective use of induction therapy (intensive immunosuppression immediately after transplant). Acute rejection requiring treatment occurs in 20% to 25% of recipients within a year, and may be treated with high-dose corticosteroids, antithymocyte globulin, and B-cell-targeted therapies (such as rituximab or bortezomib). Rejection surveillance with endomyocardial biopsies of the right ventricular septum is performed 5 to 10 times within the first year after transplant. Cardiac allograft vasculopathy (chronic rejection that causes diffuse coronary narrowing) affects 29% of heart transplant recipients within 5 years and 46.8% by 10 years and is the leading cause of late mortality. Cardiac allograft vasculopathy is treated with statins, mechanistic target of rapamycin inhibitors (such as sirolimus or everolimus), and retransplant for severe cases. Infections (62.5% are bacterial, 30.0% are viral, 6.7% are fungal, and 0.8% are parasitic) and malignancy (10.7% within 5 years after heart transplant) are other leading causes of late mortality.<h4>Conclusions and relevance</h4>Heart transplant is the definitive therapy for carefully selected patients with advanced heart failure despite receiving optimal, guideline-directed therapy. After heart transplant, lifelong multidisciplinary care is necessary to manage immunosuppression, prevent and treat rejection and cardiac allograft vasculopathy, and monitor for infection and malignancy.

Primary outcomes were any major congenital malformation and organ-specific malformations previously associated with benzodiazepine exposure. The investigators estimated relative risks (RRs) comparing exposed versus unexposed pregnancies with propensity score fine stratification weights to control for confounders. An RR of 1.3 or higher was prespecified as clinically significant.<h4>Results</h4>In total, 51 339 of 2 478 434 (2.1%) publicly insured and 27 779 of 1 750 721 (1.6%) commercially insured pregnancies had benzodiazepine exposure. The adjusted absolute risks for any malformation in exposed versus unexposed pregnancies were 4.0% versus 4.1% for Medicaid and 4.9% versus 4.8% for MarketScan. Associations with organ-specific malformations were not consistently observed in sensitivity analyses, nor was a dose-response relationship observed. Analyses of different benzodiazepines yielded similar results. The risk for selected organ-specific malformations was increased in some analyses of coexposure to benzodiazepines and other psychoactive medications, but a consistent pattern did not emerge.<h4>Limitations</h4>Potential nondifferential exposure and outcome misclassification; analyses restricted to malformations prevalent at birth.<h4>Conclusion</h4>No substantial increase in the risk for malformations was consistently identified across data sources. ### [Delirium.](https://doi.org/10.7326/annals-26-03322)

- Source: `annals_internal_abstracts` · published `2026-10-13` · freshness `stale`
- Reading value: `pending` · status: `ineligible`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `pmid:42863116`

Delirium is a common, acute confusional state that is associated with adverse patient, caregiver, and health system outcomes. All patients are at risk for delirium, although those with advanced age or acute infection and those taking sedatives and anticholinergics are at greater risk. The pathophysiology of delirium is complex and includes neuronal injury, inflammation, metabolic imbalance, and blood-brain barrier disruption. It is important to note that delirium is preventable; recognizing people at risk and identifying and addressing predisposing and precipitating factors is paramount. No single intervention or medication has been shown to be effective in treating delirium; therefore, standard-of-care treatments rely on multifaceted strategies to address contributing factors.

### [How Would You Screen This 26-Year-Old Woman for Cervical Cancer? Grand Rounds Discussion From Beth Israel Deaconess Medical Center.](https://doi.org/10.7326/annals-26-02904)

- Source: `annals_internal_abstracts` · published `2026-10-13` · freshness `stale`
- Reading value: `pending` · status: `ineligible`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `pmid:42863118`

Cervical cancer is the third most common gynecologic cancer, with more than 13 000 new cases diagnosed in 2025. The incidence of and mortality from cervical cancer vary across racial and ethnic groups and in persons living in medically underserved or geographically isolated areas, with half of all cervical cancers occurring in women who are underscreened or have never been screened. Persistent infection with high-risk types of human papillomavirus (HPV) is the most important risk factor for cervical cancer and is associated with nearly all cases. Although genital tract HPV infection is common, most HPV infections are transient, and cervical cancer occurs in only a small portion of patients with HPV. For decades, cervical cancer screening relied on annual cytologic assessment. A growing understanding of the role of HPV infection in the natural progression of cervical cancer has led to updated screening recommendations from the American Cancer Society and the Women's Preventive Services Initiative, as well as a draft update from the U.S. Preventive Services Task Force. These recommendations include primary HPV testing as a preferred screening strategy and include an option for self-collected specimens for HPV testing. Here, 2 experts in this field, a primary care physician and an obstetrics and gynecology physician, debate how to apply these guidelines when screening a patient for cervical cancer. They discuss a range of screening options and consider the challenges of applying these guidelines across different age groups and in patients at increased risk.

### [Reporting completeness of clinical trials evaluating large language model interventions in medicine: systematic review.](https://doi.org/10.1136/bmj-2026-100855)

- Source: `bmj_abstracts` · published `2026-10-13` · freshness `stale`
- Reading value: `pending` · status: `ineligible`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `pmid:42869550`

<h4>Objective</h4>To characterise the reporting completeness of clinical trials involving large language model (LLM) interventions using established reporting guidelines.<h4>Design</h4>Systematic review and meta-research analysis.<h4>Data sources</h4>Bibliographic databases (Embase, Medline, and CENTRAL) were searched for trial reports and protocols, and trial registries (ClinicalTrials.gov and World Health Organization International Clinical Trials Registry Platform) were searched for trial registrations published from 1 January 2017 to 6 December 2025.<h4>Eligibility criteria</h4>Trial reports, trial protocols, and trial registrations describing clinical trials of LLM interventions in medicine. LLM interventions used for a healthcare function in a clinical trial setting included patient education, clinical documentation, communication support, triage, care navigation, management recommendations, or clinical decision support. Eligible studies prospectively assigned human participants to the intervention and evaluated a health related biomedical or behavioural outcome.<h4>Main outcome measures</h4>Overall study level and item level reporting completeness assessed using reporting guidelines matched to study design and purpose, including TRIPOD-LLM (Transparent Reporting of a multivariable prediction model for Individual Prognosis Or Diagnosis-LLM), CONSORT (Consolidated Standards of Reporting Trials) 2025 and CONSORT-AI, SPIRIT (Standard Protocol Items: Recommendations for Interventional Trials) 2025 and SPIRIT-AI, CHART (Chatbot Assessment Reporting Tool), and the WHO Trial Registration Dataset (version 1.3.1). Two reviewers independently screened and extracted trial and intervention characteristics, and three calibrated raters assessed item level reporting completeness.<h4>Results</h4>The systematic search yielded 461 records, including 68 trial reports, 15 protocols, and 378 registrations. Trial reports were predominantly small sample, single site randomised studies published between 2024 and 2025, spanning diverse clinical specialties and primarily targeting patient facing education and management recommendations. Mean reporting completeness was modest across eligible trial reports (TRIPOD-LLM 40%, CONSORT 2025 44%, CONSORT-AI 59%, CHART 52%), trial protocols (SPIRIT 2025 51%, SPIRIT-AI 57%), and trial registrations (WHO Trial Registration Dataset 61%). Clinical trial reports of LLM interventions prioritise narrative framing and commentary over the methodological detail needed for replication, risk-of-bias assessment, and governance of model performance over time. Critical methodological elements for reproducibility, including data and intervention provenance, input preprocessing, model versioning and access modality, inference parameters, output postprocessing, as well as protocol, data, and code access, were inconsistently reported. Harms planning, monitoring, and reporting were often incompletely reported, limiting the critical appraisal of the safety of LLM interventions in healthcare.<h4>Conclusion</h4>Clinical trials of LLM interventions show modest adherence to established reporting guidelines, with persistent underreporting of LLM specific methodological details, reproducibility artefacts (protocols, data, code), and harms reporting required to support reproducibility and confident appraisal of safety risks before their clinical translation.

Adolescents with PHIV scored significantly lower than controls on global scores for both (=.008) and the traditional battery (=.003), even after adjusting for age. Agreement between batteries in classifying participants ≤1 SD (κ=0.56) and ≤2 SD (κ=0.66) below the global -score mean was moderate, with both batteries classifying more adolescents with PHIV than controls below these thresholds. Although fewer than half of participants (42/97, 43%) had previously used a tablet, most rated as easy to use, and prior tablet experience was not independently associated with performance after age adjustment. Conclusions: The Luganda-adapted demonstrated convergent, known-groups, and criterion validity in Ugandan adolescents with PHIV. By delivering automated administration, automated scoring, and standardized multimedia instructions in the local language on a portable tablet, addresses several persistent barriers to neurocognitive assessment in low-resource settings, including shortages of specialist personnel, the time burden of traditional batteries, and the scarcity of culturally and linguistically appropriate tools. The battery was found to be highly acceptable among adolescents with little or no prior tablet experience. These findings add to a growing body of evidence supporting tablet-based assessment as a practical and scalable mobile health approach to expanding access to neurocognitive testing. Future research should evaluate test-retest reliability, sensitivity to change over time, normative benchmarks, and implementation within real-world clinical workflows.

### [Bat-borne zoonoses: transmission, impact, prevention, and management.](https://doi.org/10.1136/bmj-2026-100560)

- Source: `bmj_abstracts` · published `2026-10-14` · freshness `stale`
- Reading value: `pending` · status: `ineligible`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `pmid:42875048`

Climate change and biodiversity loss are global environmental crises driving emerging infectious diseases, including bat-borne pathogens of public health significance: Ebola, Marburg, Nipah, Hendra, classic rabies virus, and Australian bat lyssavirus, as well as the fungus <i>Histoplasma</i> This review presents the evidence on the mechanisms of transmission, impact, and prevention and management strategies relevant to each of these pathogens. Transmission pathways vary between pathogens but often involve exposure to body fluids or excreta of a bat or other infected animal or human. The diseases associated with these infections have high mortality rates, and survivors may continue to experience a range of long term effects, including neurological and neuropsychiatric signs and symptoms. Prevention strategies may be implemented to reduce the risk of zoonotic spillover and human-to-human transmission of these pathogens, including better ecological stewardship, reducing exposure of domestic animals and humans to bats, isolation of infected people, and, where possible, vaccination of humans and animals. The mainstay of treatment for these conditions is supportive care; however, specific drugs are now available for histoplasmosis and <i>Ebolavirus</i> infection. In the face of climate change and further loss of biodiversity, developing more effective strategies to prevent and manage these conditions will be critical.

<h4>Background</h4>Continuous glucose monitoring (CGM) has been shown to be beneficial in persons with type 1 diabetes and others with insulin-treated type 2 diabetes, but its role in the treatment of persons with type 2 diabetes not being treated with insulin has needed further study.<h4>Methods</h4>In a 26-week trial conducted at 22 primary care practices in the United States, we randomly assigned adults with type 2 diabetes who were not using insulin to either CGM or routine care (control). The primary outcome was the glycated hemoglobin level at 26 weeks. Key CGM-measured secondary outcomes were tested hierarchically to control the type I error rate.<h4>Results</h4>A total of 145 participants were assigned to the CGM group, and 138 to the routine-care group. Participants ranged in age from 21 to 84 years. The mean (±SD) glycated hemoglobin level decreased from 8.8±1.3% at baseline to 7.2±1.1% at 26 weeks in the CGM group and from 8.7±1.2% to 8.0±1.4% in the routine-care group (adjusted mean difference, -0.9 percentage points; 95% confidence interval [CI], -1.1 to -0.6; P<0.001). The mean percentage of time that the CGM-measured glucose level was in the glucose-level range of 70 to 180 mg per deciliter was 21 percentage points higher (95% CI, 16 to 26) with CGM than with routine care (P<0.001). The between-group difference in the percentage of time that the glucose level was below 54 mg per deciliter was -0.17 percentage points (95% CI, -0.27 to -0.11; P<0.001 for noninferiority); the difference in the percentage of time that the glucose level was below 70 mg per deciliter was -0.20 percentage points (95% CI, -0.43 to -0.09; P<0.001 for noninferiority; P = 0.004 for superiority). No severe hypoglycemia events were reported.<h4>Conclusions</h4>Among adults with type 2 diabetes that was not being treated with insulin and was being managed in a primary care setting, the use of CGM led to a greater reduction in the glycated hemoglobin level and to a lower risk of hyperglycemia than routine care. (Funded by Dexcom; CONNECT ClinicalTrials.gov number, NCT06601166.).

<h4>Importance</h4>The optimal vasopressor dose at which to initiate a second vasopressor in patients with septic shock is uncertain.<h4>Objective</h4>To compare the effects of hospital-level strategies initiating vasopressin infusion at a lower threshold vs a higher threshold on outcomes in patients with septic shock.<h4>Design, setting, and participants</h4>Cluster-randomized, cluster-crossover, pragmatic clinical strategy trial including adult patients receiving vasopressors for septic shock at 13 hospitals in Idaho and Utah from February 1, 2024, to July 31, 2025. The last day of follow-up for the primary outcome was February 17, 2026.<h4>Intervention</h4>Hospital-level implementation of septic shock management strategies recommending fixed-dose vasopressin initiation when the total dose of other vasopressors reached 0.1 µg/kg/min of norepinephrine or equivalent (lower-threshold strategy) or when the total dose of other vasopressors reached 0.4 µg/kg/min of norepinephrine or equivalent (higher-threshold strategy). Hospitals were randomized for the first month to an initial strategy (6 hospitals to the lower threshold vs 7 hospitals to the higher threshold) and then crossed over to the alternate strategy monthly thereafter.<h4>Main outcomes and measures</h4>The primary outcome of 28-day mortality and the key secondary outcome of kidney replacement therapy-free days through day 28 were analyzed using multivariable generalized linear mixed-effects models, accounting for trial site and baseline patient characteristics.<h4>Results</h4>There were 3959 unique patients with septic shock (median age, 68 [IQR, 57 to 77] years; 1903 [48%] were female). Of the patients more likely to experience differential management under the tested hospital-level vasopressin dose strategies (maximum vasopressor dose ≥0.07 µg/kg/min of norepinephrine or equivalent), 1034 of 1521 (68.0%) cared for under the lower-threshold strategy received vasopressin compared with 651 of 1526 (42.7%) cared for under the higher-threshold strategy. In the hospital-level strategy recommending a lower-dose threshold for vasopressin initiation, the maximum vasopressor dose received prior to vasopressin initiation was a median of 0.14 (IQR, 0.10 to 0.22) µg/kg/min of norepinephrine or equivalent vs 0.34 (IQR, 0.16 to 0.42) µg/kg/min of norepinephrine or equivalent in the hospital-level strategy recommending a higher-dose threshold for vasopressin initiation. Mortality at 28 days occurred in 628 of 1975 patients (31.8%) treated with the hospital-level strategy recommending initiation of vasopressin at a lower-dose threshold of other vasopressors vs 655 of 1984 patients (33.0%) treated with the higher-threshold strategy (adjusted marginal odds ratio, 0.98 [95% CI, 0.87 to 1.06], P = .62; adjusted risk difference, -0.4 [95% CI, -2.7 to 1.3] percentage points). Patients in both strategies for vasopressin initiation had a median of 29 (IQR, -1 to 29) kidney replacement therapy-free days (adjusted marginal odds ratio, 1.01 [95% CI, 0.91 to 1.14]).<h4>Conclusions and relevance</h4>In this large, pragmatic clinical trial comparing the effectiveness of 2 hospital-level strategies for second-line vasopressor management in septic shock, a strategy recommending initiation of vasopressin at a lower dose of other vasopressors did not reduce 28-day mortality compared with a strategy recommending vasopressin initiation at a higher dose of other vasopressors.<h4>Trial registration</h4>ClinicalTrials.gov Identifier: NCT06217562.

### [Evaluation of the accuracy, completeness, and compliance of current summary reporting of adverse events to the Food and Drug Administration using the manufacturer and user facility device experience (MAUDE) database: cross sectional study.](https://doi.org/10.1136/bmj-2026-100812)

- Source: `bmj_abstracts` · published `2026-10-14` · freshness `stale`
- Reading value: `pending` · status: `ineligible`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `pmid:42875046`

<h4>Objectives</h4>To evaluate the accuracy and completeness of adverse event summary reports for medical devices and compliance of the reports with the Food and Drug Administration (FDA) requirements.<h4>Design</h4>Cross sectional study.<h4>Setting</h4>The FDA manufacturer and user facility device experience (MAUDE) database.<h4>Participants</h4>All adverse event summary reports filed in MAUDE from July 2019 to June 2025.<h4>Main outcome measures</h4>Identification of device problem codes, appropriate identification as summary reports in the submission form, concordance between the number of events data field and the report narrative, and compliance with FDA reporting deadlines.<h4>Results</h4>6308 summary reports were submitted to MAUDE containing 159 503 adverse events. Use of summary reports increased from 808 to 1757 between 2020 and 2024, a factor of 1.21 per year (P<0.001). Most (91%) adverse events in summary reports were for class 3 high risk devices. Summary reports lacked descriptions of potential device related problems: 57% of reports denoted an "adverse event without identified device or use problem" and 38% of reports selected "insufficient information" in the device problem code data field. Manufacturers incorrectly checked "N" (no) in the summary report (Y/N, reporting yes/no) field for 694 summary reports (11%) and erroneously listed "n=1" under the number of events field for 423 summary reports (7%), causing an undercount of 11 902 adverse events. The majority (55%) of summary reports failed to comply with the FDA reporting deadline of 30 days after the manufacturer became aware of the adverse event.<h4>Conclusions</h4>Adverse event summary reports in MAUDE are frequently incomplete, inaccurate, and delayed. Improvements are needed to ensure timely identification of device safety signals and uphold FDA transparency standards.

### [Fluid Therapy in Mechanically Ventilated Critically Ill Adult Patients.](https://doi.org/10.1001/jama.2026.21291)

- Source: `jama_abstracts` · published `2026-10-14` · freshness `stale`
- Reading value: `pending` · status: `ineligible`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `pmid:42872606`

<h4>Importance</h4>Fluid therapy is ubiquitous in the intensive care unit (ICU) but carries risks, especially in mechanically ventilated patients. Contemporary global practice across dose, indication, and type is unknown.<h4>Objective</h4>To characterize ICU fluid practices in mechanically ventilated adults.<h4>Design, setting, and participants</h4>International prospective observational cohort study between April 2024 and July 2025, across 206 ICUs in 41 countries on 6 continents. Consecutive adult patients receiving invasive mechanical ventilation within 3 days of ICU admission and ventilated 24 hours or more. Exclusion criteria were age younger than 16 years and transfer under mechanical ventilation from another ICU.<h4>Exposures</h4>Fluid volume, indication, and type after intubation.<h4>Main outcomes and measures</h4>Key measures included (1) total infused volume and daily fluid balance over 7 days after intubation; (2) the indication for fluid therapy over the first 3 days after intubation; (3) the proportion of total administered fluid volume attributable to each fluid type over the first 3 days after intubation.<h4>Results</h4>Among 2454 patients analyzed (median age, 63 years [IQR, 49-73 years]; 63.6% [1560] male), median daily fluid input peaked on day 1 at 3.0 L (IQR, 2.2-4.0 L) and declined to 2.6 L (IQR, 1.8-3.5 L) through day 6. Daily fluid balance (median, 0.9 L; IQR, 0.1-1.9 L on day 1) decreased to less than 0.3 L (IQR, -0.5 to 1.2 L) by day 3 and remained near-neutral through day 6. Maintenance and replacement fluids (to meet daily requirements and to correct ongoing losses; 31.9%) and creep fluids (to deliver medications and maintain catheter patency; 30.9%) were the primary indications, whereas resuscitation (to restore circulating blood volume; 16.0%) accounted for a smaller proportion. Isotonic balanced crystalloids (48.9%) and isotonic saline (0.9% sodium chloride) (27.0%) were common; colloids were rare (1.3%). Substantial between-site variation persisted after adjustment for case-mix.<h4>Conclusions and relevance</h4>In this prospective international cohort study, fluid therapy varied widely among mechanically ventilated critically ill patients, with maintenance and creep fluids-rather than resuscitation fluids-accounting for most total volume.

<h4>Background</h4>The preferred method of renal-replacement therapy for acute kidney injury in critically ill patients remains uncertain.<h4>Methods</h4>In this multicenter, open-label, noninferiority trial, we randomly assigned critically ill adults with acute kidney injury who had indications for renal-replacement therapy to undergo intermittent hemodialysis or continuous renal-replacement therapy as the initial treatment. The primary outcome was a major adverse kidney event - defined as death, dependence on renal-replacement therapy, or a serum creatinine concentration more than 25% above the baseline value - by day 90.<h4>Results</h4>The trial was stopped early after sequential interim analyses showed a sustained signal of higher mortality with intermittent hemodialysis than with continuous renal-replacement therapy. A total of 554 patients underwent randomization, with 272 patients assigned to undergo intermittent hemodialysis and 282 to undergo continuous renal-replacement therapy; 535 patients (260 in the intermittent group and 275 in the continuous group) were included in the intention-to-treat population. A major adverse kidney event occurred by day 90 in 203 patients (78.1%) in the intermittent group and in 197 patients (71.6%) in the continuous group (adjusted absolute risk difference, 6.28 percentage points; 95% confidence interval [CI], -1.22 to 13.79). Death by day 90 occurred in 164 patients (63.1%) and in 147 patients (53.5%), respectively. Kaplan-Meier mortality estimates at day 90 were 63.3% and 53.6% (hazard ratio, 1.30; 95% CI, 1.04 to 1.63). The most common complication potentially related to acute kidney injury or renal-replacement therapy was hypotension, which occurred in 179 patients (68.8%) in the intermittent group and in 183 patients (66.5%) in the continuous group.<h4>Conclusions</h4>Among critically ill patients with acute kidney injury who had indications for renal-replacement therapy, intermittent hemodialysis as the initial treatment approach was not shown to be noninferior to continuous renal-replacement therapy with respect to major adverse kidney events by day 90 and was associated with higher mortality. (Funded by the French Ministry of Health; ICRAKI ClinicalTrials.gov number, NCT06032884.).

No significant differences were observed in 9 of the 11 secondary outcomes. SOFA scores on postoperative day 1 (adjusted difference, -0.53 [95% CI, -0.98 to -0.07]) were lower and urine output 1 hour post intervention (adjusted difference, 0.55 [95% CI, 0.03 to 1.07] mL/kg/h) was higher in the dobutamine plus norepinephrine group. Stage 2 and 3 AKI were lower in the dobutamine plus norepinephrine group compared with the norepinephrine alone group (stage 2: 4.8% vs 9.9%; ARR, 0.48 [95% CI, 0.26-0.88]; stage 3: 7.9% vs 13.6%; ARR, 0.56 [95% CI, 0.35-0.91]). Tachycardia (heart rate >100/min) occurred more frequently in patients receiving dobutamine and norepinephrine than norepinephrine alone.<h4>Conclusions and relevance</h4>Among adult patients with septic shock undergoing surgical source control, intraoperative dobutamine combined with norepinephrine reduced the incidence of new-onset or worsening AKI within 7 postoperative days compared with norepinephrine.<h4>Trial registration</h4>ClinicalTrials.gov Identifier: NCT06462313.

<h4>Importance</h4>Rigorous evidence for inhaled sedation using isoflurane in mechanically ventilated patients with acute hypoxemic respiratory failure (AHRF) is limited.<h4>Objective</h4>To evaluate whether an inhaled sedation strategy lowers hospital mortality compared with usual care intravenous sedation strategy in mechanically ventilated adults with AHRF.<h4>Design, setting, and participants</h4>Pragmatic, effectiveness, open-label, superiority, randomized clinical trial in 14 intensive care units (ICUs) in Canada. Adults (≥18 years) receiving sedation for less than 72 hours and receiving mechanical ventilation for AHRF from non-COVID respiratory failure (ratio of partial pressure of arterial oxygen [Pao2] to fraction of inspired oxygen [Fio2] <300 mm Hg) or COVID-19 were enrolled between September 2020 and March 2025 with follow-up through June 2025.<h4>Intervention</h4>Patients were randomized to an inhaled sedation strategy using isoflurane with intravenous adjuncts as clinically needed or to a usual intravenous sedation care strategy. Sedatives were titrated to achieve the desired target sedation score guided by clinical teams and continued until extubation or death.<h4>Main outcomes and measures</h4>Primary outcome was all-cause hospital mortality. Secondary outcomes included ICU- and ventilator-free days at day 30; hospital-free days at day 60; use of refractory hypoxemia adjunctive therapies; delirium- and coma-free days at day 14; and quality-of-life and disability scores at 3 months.<h4>Results</h4>Of 786 patients randomized, 780 were included in the primary analysis (mean age, 61.1 years; 34.0% female; 73.7% non-COVID AHRF, mean Pao2:Fio2 ratio, 152.6 mm Hg); 385 in the inhaled sedation and 395 in the intravenous sedation groups. In the inhaled sedation group, 49.9% to 55.4% of patients received concomitant intravenous sedation on any day during the first 14 days after randomization. At hospital discharge, 161 of 379 patients (42.5%) in the inhaled sedation group had died, compared with 171 of 390 patients (43.8%) in the intravenous sedation group (risk ratio, 0.98 [95% CI, 0.84 to 1.14]; P = .85). There was no effect modification in prespecified subgroup analyses for age, sex, and COVID-19 status. ICU-, ventilator-, and hospital-free days were similar between groups. The inhaled sedation group received more adjunctive therapies for hypoxemia (239 [63.9%] vs 214 [55.0%]; between-group difference, 8.89% [95% CI, 1.95% to 15.83%]) and had fewer delirium- and coma-free days (between-group difference, -0.95 [95% CI, -1.69 to -0.22]). There was no difference in quality-of-life and disability scores between groups.<h4>Conclusions and relevance</h4>In mechanically ventilated adults with AHRF, an inhaled sedation strategy using isoflurane with intravenous adjuncts as clinically needed did not reduce hospital mortality compared with an intravenous sedation strategy.<h4>Trial registration</h4>ClinicalTrials.gov Identifier: NCT04415060.

### [Clinician Use of an Artificial Intelligence-Enabled Clinical Information Platform.](https://doi.org/10.1001/jama.2026.21868)

- Source: `jama_abstracts` · published `2026-10-15` · freshness `stale`
- Reading value: `pending` · status: `ineligible`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `pmid:42879271`

<h4>Importance</h4>Despite growing interest in the use of artificial intelligence (AI) in medicine, empirical evidence on how clinicians use these tools is limited.<h4>Objective</h4>To characterize clinician use of an AI-enabled clinical information platform, the distribution of use across the clinical workforce, and the purpose of submitted queries.<h4>Design, setting, and participants</h4>This cross-sectional study analyzed clinicians with National Provider Identifier-verified accounts of a commonly used AI-enabled clinical information platform, OpenEvidence, from January 1, 2024, through June 30, 2026. A large language model was used to classify the clinical purpose and topic areas of a stratified random sample of 38.8 million queries.<h4>Main outcomes and measures</h4>The number of active users and clinical queries, the share of queries relative to representation in the national workforce, and the clinical purpose and topic of queries were examined.<h4>Results</h4>Overall, 952 008 clinicians (58.5% female), including 556 200 physicians, submitted 271 million queries during the 2.5-year study period. Monthly active users increased from 355 in January 2024 to 703 265 in June 2026, and average monthly queries per active user rose from 18.0 to 39.5. Nurse practitioners (44.5) and physician assistants (44.5) submitted more queries per active user-month than physicians (37.1). A higher share of queries relative to the national workforce was submitted by physicians within a decade of graduation (use ratio, 2.54) and residents and fellows (1.84). Use was also higher than average among physicians specializing in oncology and hematology (use ratio, 2.00), internal medicine (1.88), and family medicine (1.80), but lower in procedural and diagnostic specialties (ophthalmology, 0.23; radiology, 0.24; orthopedics, 0.24). Most queries addressed clinical decision-making (51.9%), particularly treatment and management (33.1%), whereas diagnosis accounted for 13.9% of queries. Queries addressing topics outside physicians' own specialties ranged from 35% among oncologists and hematologists to 88% among emergency department physicians.<h4>Conclusions and relevance</h4>Over 2.5 years, more than 950 000 US clinicians submitted greater than 270 million queries to an AI-enabled clinical information platform. Use varied across clinician types, specialties, and career stages and was frequently for treatment decisions rather than diagnostic tasks.

Background: Telemedicine has emerged as a critical strategy to address health care access gaps in hard-to-reach areas, particularly in resource-constrained settings. While telemedicine services have expanded across India, evidence on post-implementation experiences, specifically regarding the integration of portable diagnostic tools, and the assisting role of frontline health care workers, remains limited. Understanding these sociotechnical dynamics is essential for optimizing digital health interventions in remote areas. Objective: This study aimed to explore perceptions, experiences, and implementation processes of an assisted telemedicine model where frontline health care workers facilitate real-time, doorstep patient-assisted-to-doctor consultations using portable diagnostics and a customized portal among health care workers and patients in hard-to-reach villages of Rajasthan, India. Methods: A qualitative descriptive study was conducted in 5 remote villages of Balotra district, Rajasthan, between December 2025 and January 2026, following a 10-month telemedicine intervention. In-depth, semistructured interviews were conducted with 28 participants, including medical officers (n=3, 10.71%), nurses, and accredited social health activists or auxiliary nurse midwives (n=10, 35.71%), and patients (n=15, 53.57%). Cadre-specific semistructured interview guides were developed using the Consolidated Framework for Implementation Research and Technology Acceptance Model. Interviews were analyzed using a reflexive thematic analysis approach, incorporating both inductive and deductive coding approaches in NVivo 15 (Lumivero). Triangulation across participant groups and analytic perspectives was used to enhance credibility and trustworthiness. Results: The analysis revealed that the assisted telemedicine model fundamentally reconfigured treatment-seeking behavior by providing doorstep care, significantly reducing travel burdens, and out-of-pocket expenditures. Frontline health care workers acted as crucial sociotechnical mediators, bridging the digital divide and fostering patient trust, particularly among women and older adults. Health care providers reported increased clinical confidence and workflow efficiency, supported by real-time vital monitoring and cross-verification via digital chatbots. However, implementation was challenged by intermittent network connectivity, diagnostic limitations for conditions requiring physical palpation, and occasional stock-outs of essential medicines. Despite these barriers, the gradual acceptance of the system was heavily facilitated by the continuous engagement and cultural mediation of local health workers. Conclusions: Telemedicine was perceived as a feasible and acceptable model for extending primary health care services in hard-to-reach areas, provided it is embedded within an assisted model supported by trained frontline workers and responsive technical systems. The findings emphasize the need to shift health care policy toward mediated-digital models, formally recognizing community health workers as digital health navigators. Future scale-up must prioritize strengthening rural digital infrastructure and ensuring a reliable supply chain for essential medicines to sustain long-term engagement and health equity. Trial Registration: Clinical Trials Registry—India (CTRI) CTRI/2024/10/076070; https://tinyurl.com/yr952847 International Registered Report Identifier (IRRID): RR2-10.2196/92996

### [Medicare Program; Implementation of Sections 6219, 6220, and 6224 of the Consolidated Appropriations Act, 2026 (CMS-4214-P)](https://www.cms.gov/newsroom/fact-sheets/medicare-program-implementation-sections-6219-6220-6224-consolidated-appropriations-act-2026-cms)

- Source: `cms_fact_sheets` · published `2026-10-16` · freshness `stale`
- Reading value: `pending` · status: `ineligible`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `url:https://www.cms.gov/newsroom/fact-sheets/medicare-program-implementation-sections-6219-6220-6224-consolidated-appropriations-act-2026-cms`

Medicare Program; Implementation of Sections 6219, 6220, and 6224 of the Consolidated Appropriations Act, 2026 (CMS-4214-P) Background On Oct. 16, 2026, the Centers for Medicare & Medicaid Services (CMS) issued a proposed rule to implement key sections of the Consolidated Appropriations Act of 2026 including section 6220 (Requiring Enhanced and Accurate Lists of (REAL) Health Providers Act) and section 6224 (Modernizing and Ensuring Pharmacy Benefit Manager Accountability). This rule also proposes a conforming change mandated by section 6219 of the CAA (Adjustments to Medicare Part D Cost-Sharing Reductions for Low-Income Individuals) that would reduce the generic drug copayment from $1 to $0 for full-benefit dual eligible individuals. Implementing Certain Provisions of Consolidated Appropriations Act, 2026 On February 3, 2026, the CAA of 2026, which included several changes to the MA and Part D programs, was signed into law. In this rule, we are proposing to implement the following sections: Adjustments to Medicare Part D Cost-Sharing Reductions for Low-Income Individuals (Section 6219): This proposed rule implements Section 6219 of the CAA that would reduce the generic drug copayment from $1 to $0 for full-benefit dual eligible individuals at or below 100% of the Federal Poverty Level, effective for plan years starting Jan. 1, 2028. Requiring Enhanced and Accurate Lists of (REAL) Health Providers Act (Section 6220): CMS proposes to codify regulations to implement the REAL Health Providers Act, which establishes new provider directory verification and accuracy requirements for MA organizations beginning in contract year 2028. Specifically, CMS proposes requirements to implement this new law related to provider directory verification and maintenance standards, annual accuracy analysis and reporting, public reporting of accuracy scores, and cost-sharing protections for enrollees who rely on inaccurate provider directory information. CMS also proposes to codify the statutory requirements for contract year 2029, including that CMS must publicly display provider directory accuracy scores in a machine-readable format, and MA organizations must prominently display those scores in their provider directories. Through these proposed codifications implementing the REAL Health Providers Act, CMS seeks to promote provider directory accuracy and accountability among MA organizations to ensure that beneficiaries are provided with clear, accurate information on the network of providers available to meet their healthcare needs. Modernizing and Ensuring Pharmacy Benefit Manager Accountability (Section 6224): CMS has several proposals to implement section 6224 of the CAA, which established requirements related to pharmacy benefit managers (PBMs) and their affiliates specific to the Medicare Part D program. These proposals include new requirements governing the remuneration that PBMs and their affiliates may receive for services in connection with the utilization of Part D drugs, data reporting by PBMs to Part D prescription drug plan (PDP) sponsors and CMS, transparency in PBM price guarantee arrangements, disclosure by PBMs to PDP sponsors of the terms of certain agreements between PBMs or their affiliates and pharmaceutical manufacturers, and other requirements added by the CAA. CMS also proposes to define key terms related to such requirements and establish compliance and enforcement mechanisms, including disgorgement of funds to CMS for violations of the remuneration requirements. The proposed rule will be open for public comment for 60 days following publication in the Federal Register. View this regulation on the Federal Register . The proposed rule will be open for public comment for 60 days following publication in the Federal Register. ### CMS News and Media Group Catherine Howden, Director Media Inquiries Form 202-690-6145

### [A Common CD36 Variant and the Genetic Landscape of Dilated Cardiomyopathy in Individuals of African Ancestry.](https://doi.org/10.1001/jama.2026.18449)

- Source: `jama_abstracts` · published `2026-10-19` · freshness `stale`
- Reading value: `pending` · status: `ineligible`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `pmid:42895716`

<h4>Importance</h4>Dilated cardiomyopathy (DCM) is a major cause of heart failure that disproportionately affects individuals of predominantly African ancestry, among whom familial clustering of disease is more pronounced relative to those of predominantly European ancestry. Truncating variants in TTN, which encodes the sarcomeric protein titin, are the most common established monogenic cause of DCM. A common CD36 nonsense variant enriched among individuals of African ancestry has also been shown to account for a substantial burden of DCM.<h4>Objective</h4>To compare the contribution of a CD36 nonsense variant to DCM risk with that of truncating variants in TTN and pathogenic or likely pathogenic variants in other established genes for DCM.<h4>Design, setting, and participants</h4>Multicohort genetic association study including participants of African and European ancestry with exome or genome sequence data and DCM case status from the following 4 US datasets: All of Us Research Program (recruitment: 2017-present; last follow-up: August 27, 2024), Million Veteran Program (recruitment: 2011-present; last follow-up: September 30, 2024), Penn Medicine Biobank (recruitment: 2013-present; last follow-up: November 14, 2022), and the DCM Precision Medicine Study (recruitment: 2016-2020; last follow-up: March 15, 2020).<h4>Exposure</h4>Carrier status for TTN truncating variants, pathogenic or likely pathogenic variants in 11 high-confidence genes for DCM, and the nonsense variant CD36 Y325* (0, 1, or 2 copies).<h4>Main outcomes and measures</h4>Risk of DCM, prevalence of variant carriers among DCM cases, and population attributable fraction of DCM.<h4>Results</h4>Among 82 623 individuals of predominantly African ancestry across 4 cohorts, the mean age was 53.4 years (SD, 13.9 years), 47 124 were male, and 1316 had DCM. Individuals who were homozygous for the CD36 Y325* risk allele had a higher risk of DCM (odds ratio [OR], 4.8; 95% CI, 3.1-7.3) as did those who were heterozygous (OR, 1.4; 95% CI, 1.2-1.7). TTN truncating variants also conferred an elevated risk of DCM in participants of African ancestry (OR, 8.5; 95% CI, 5.5-13.0). Among 1316 individuals of African ancestry classified as DCM cases, 33 (2.5%) were homozygous for CD36, 56 (4.3%) had TTN truncating variants, and 16 (1.2%) had pathogenic or likely pathogenic variants in other high-confidence genes for DCM. In population-level analyses incorporating both heterozygous and homozygous CD36 Y325* carriers, the population attributable fraction for CD36 (9.0% [6.2% for heterozygous carriers and 2.8% for homozygous carriers]) surpassed the population attributable fraction for TTN truncating variants (3.7%).<h4>Conclusions and relevance</h4>In 4 US cohorts, an ancestry-specific CD36 variant contributed more to DCM burden among individuals of African ancestry than established genes for DCM, including TTN truncating variants that are typically considered the most common genetic cause. These findings reshape the known genetic architecture of DCM in individuals of African ancestry and highlight the importance of representation in genomic research.

<h4>Importance</h4>It remains unclear if intraoperative hemodynamic therapy should be guided by cardiac index.<h4>Objective</h4>To evaluate whether individualized cardiac index-guided hemodynamic therapy vs routine hemodynamic therapy improves outcomes in high-risk patients undergoing elective major abdominal surgery.<h4>Design, setting, and participants</h4>This multicenter, single-blind, superiority randomized clinical trial enrolled high-risk patients undergoing elective major abdominal surgery between October 8, 2023, and October 25, 2025, at 12 university medical centers in Austria, the Czech Republic, Denmark, Germany, and Spain. The date of last follow-up was January 15, 2026.<h4>Interventions</h4>Patients were randomized to individualized cardiac index-guided hemodynamic therapy targeting the patient's preoperative resting cardiac index vs routine hemodynamic therapy (with blinded cardiac index monitoring).<h4>Main outcomes and measures</h4>The primary outcome was a composite of acute kidney injury, acute myocardial injury, severe infectious complications, nonfatal cardiac arrest, or death within the first 7 postoperative days. Secondary outcomes included the individual components of the primary outcome within the first 7 postoperative days.<h4>Results</h4>Of the 1128 patients enrolled, 1111 were randomized and 1095 were included in the primary analysis (median age, 67 years [IQR, 59-74 years]; 49.4% were female). The primary outcome occurred in 243 of 549 patients (44.3%) in the individualized cardiac index-guided hemodynamic therapy group vs 256 of 546 patients (46.9%) in the routine hemodynamic therapy group (relative risk, 0.94 [95.1% CI, 0.83-1.08]; P = .38). There were no between-group differences for the incidence of the individual components of the primary outcome within the first 7 postoperative days.<h4>Conclusions and relevance</h4>Among high-risk patients undergoing elective major abdominal surgery, individualized cardiac index-guided hemodynamic therapy targeting the patient's preoperative resting cardiac index did not reduce the incidence of a composite outcome of acute kidney injury, acute myocardial injury, severe infectious complications, nonfatal cardiac arrest, or death within the first 7 postoperative days compared with routine hemodynamic therapy.<h4>Trial registration</h4>ClinicalTrials.gov Identifier: NCT05648279.
