### [Efficacy and cost effectiveness of high volume injections with and without corticosteroid compared with sham for midportion Achilles tendinopathy (Inject trial): randomised controlled trial.](https://doi.org/10.1136/bmj-2026-100807)

- Source: `bmj_abstracts` · published `2026-10-28` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `pending` · status: `failed`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `pmid:42951276`

<h4>Objective</h4>To test the efficacy and cost effectiveness of high volume injections with and without corticosteroid versus sham injection for midportion Achilles tendinopathy.<h4>Design</h4>Single centred, participant and assessor masked, three arm randomised controlled trial (Inject trial).<h4>Setting</h4>Community.<h4>Participants</h4>From 5 December 2019 and 24 July 2022, 192 adults (18 years or older) diagnosed with midportion Achilles tendinopathy were randomly assigned (1:1:1) to sham (n=64), high volume injection without corticosteroid (n=63), or high volume injection with corticosteroid (n=65).<h4>Interventions</h4>Participants were allocated to sham injection (2-3 mL 0.5% bupivacaine hydrochloride), high volume injection with corticosteroid (50 mL saline and 0.5% bupivacaine with 25 mg hydrocortisone in the first 10 mL), or high volume injection with no corticosteroid (same solution but without hydrocortisone). They received the same exercise programme, education, and activity modification advice.<h4>Main outcome measures</h4>The primary clinical outcome was pain and disability using the validated VISA-A and the primary endpoint was at 52 weeks. The economic outcome was cost per quality-adjusted life-years from a societal perspective.<h4>Results</h4>VISA-A data were obtained from 181 (94%) of 192 participants at 12 months. No clear differences were noted between sham and high volume injection either with (-3.5 (98.33% confidence interval -12.0 to 5.0)) or without corticosteroid (4.0 (-4.2 to 12.3)), and between the two injections (-7.5 (-15.3 to 0.3)), at 12 months. The point estimate was closest to the target difference for the comparison between injections and favoured the arm without steroid. The only differences observed on VISA-A at intermediate time points were a benefit for high volume with corticosteroid versus sham at six weeks (9.2 (1.5 to 16.9)) but worse outcomes for high volume injection with corticosteroid versus high volume injection with no corticosteroid at 26 weeks (-8.6 (-16.4 to -0.7)). The health economic analysis found high volume injection with corticosteroid to be less costly than the sham with similar effectiveness based on quality-adjusted life-years (>0.90 probability of being cost effective at $AUD50 000 and $100 000 willingness-to-pay thresholds). No serious trial related adverse events were recorded.<h4>Conclusion</h4>High volume injections with or without corticosteroids did not improve pain or disability compared with sham at 12 months. Although high volume injections with corticosteroid showed short term clinical benefit and potential reduction in healthcare costs over a year, they were associated with worse longer term clinical outcomes. The clinical data do not support routine use for high volume injections with corticosteroid for midportion Achilles tendinopathy.<h4>Trial registration</h4>Australian New Zealand Clinical Trials Registry ACTRN12619001455156.

### [Emotional and Literacy Adaptation in Large Language Model–Based Medical Guidance: Development and Usability Study](https://medinform.jmir.org/2026/1/e100369)

- Source: `jmir_medinform` · published `2026-10-28` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `pending` · status: `failed`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `url:https://medinform.jmir.org/2026/1/e100369`

<strong>Background:</strong> Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly deployed as conversational agents for communication and guidance, but their ability to adapt responses to a patient’s emotional state and literacy level remains insufficiently characterized. In patient-facing scenarios, effective communication requires not only accurate medical content but also appropriate tone, clarity, and responsiveness to user vulnerability. <strong>Objective:</strong> This study aimed to evaluate, within a controlled, single-scenario experimental design, the ability of contemporary LLMs to adapt medical guidance responses to different levels of emotional sensitivity and literacy, and to assess their consistency in both generating and interpreting such adaptations. <strong>Methods:</strong> A controlled benchmark was designed to evaluate how 4 LLMs adapt medical guidance responses to predefined emotional and literacy levels for a realistic patient profile asking an open-ended question. To establish a robust performance baseline, one model was deployed locally (Gemma 3 27B), and 3 are cloud-based (Claude Opus 4.5, Gemini 3 Pro, and GPT 5.2). Each model was tested across all combinations of 5 emotional levels, 3 literacy levels, and 3 prompt-verbosity variants, yielding 45 responses per model. Generated responses were assessed against human-reviewed ground truth for emotional and literacy alignment. A separate self-evaluation procedure measured each model’s ability to interpret its own outputs using the same adaptation framework. The study also examined generation-interpretation consistency and quantitative text properties, with particular attention to response length and verbosity. <strong>Results:</strong> Literacy adaptation was consistently accurate across models, often achieving near-zero error, indicating strong capability in adjusting explanatory complexity. In contrast, emotional adaptation showed greater variability and was more sensitive to model architecture and prompt design. The larger cloud-based models demonstrated more stable performance overall, while the local model exhibited higher variability, particularly under shorter prompts. A systematic asymmetry was observed between generation and interpretation. The models frequently misclassified low emotional and literacy levels as “normal,” suggesting limitations in recognizing user needs. Quantitative analysis revealed that response length increased with both emotional sensitivity and literacy level, with prompt verbosity and model-specific tendencies acting as secondary factors. <strong>Conclusions:</strong> Within our scenario, current LLMs demonstrated the capacity to support adaptive medical guidance, but their performance was uneven across dimensions, especially in terms of emotional generation and interpretation. For health care applications, these systems should be treated as assistive tools under human oversight rather than as autonomous communicators, with careful consideration of ethical, privacy, and safety implications in sensitive health contexts. <strong>Trial Registration:</strong>

### [Harnessing AI for Hypothyroidism Monitoring Using Wearable-Derived Heart Rate Data: Retrospective Cohort Study](https://ai.jmir.org/2026/1/e86030/)

- Source: `jmir_ai` · published `2026-10-28` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `pending` · status: `failed`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `url:https://ai.jmir.org/2026/1/e86030/`

Background: Hypothyroidism is a prevalent endocrine disorder characterized by an insidious onset and nonspecific symptoms, often leading to significant diagnostic delays. While biochemical thyroid function tests (TFTs) are the gold standard, their invasive nature and cost make them impractical for continuous, proactive screening of at-risk populations. Thyroid hormones exert direct chronotropic effects on the cardiovascular system, and their levels are closely correlated with heart rate (HR). Wearable devices capable of continuous HR monitoring offer a novel opportunity for noninvasive, digital phenotype–based detection. Objective: This study aimed to develop and validate an AI-assisted system that uses wearable-derived sleep HR data as a digital monitoring tool for individuals at risk of hypothyroidism, helping identify those who warrant further confirmatory thyroid testing. Methods: This retrospective cohort study used 1677 paired observations consisting of sleep HR data and serum TFT results, including free thyroxine (T4) and thyroid-stimulating hormone levels, from 413 participants (mean age 43.1, SD 9.7 y; women: n=314, 76%). Participants wore consumer-grade wearables (Fitbit) that recorded HR and sleep data. Each TFT result was paired with sleep HR data extracted from the 15 consecutive nights preceding the blood test. An AI system was developed using a gradient boosting algorithm (LightGBM) to classify thyroid status by integrating baseline TFT results with statistical and temporal HR features. The model’s performance was evaluated using patient-level leave-one-out cross-validation. To examine the benefit of longitudinal data, performance was compared using 1, 2, or 3 prior paired observations as reference points. Results: To ensure clinical relevance and avoid performance inflation from hyperthyroid cases, we prioritized the analysis of a subset containing only euthyroid and hypothyroid data (n=744 paired observations). In this clinically challenging subset, the system achieved an accuracy of 74.6%, with a sensitivity of 82% and a specificity of 73.5% using a single-reference data pair. Incorporating longitudinal HR patterns further enhanced performance; using 3 reference pairs improved accuracy to 78.1% (sensitivity 81.8% and specificity 77.7%). In the overall dataset (n=1677 paired observations), the system demonstrated an accuracy of 80.8%, with a sensitivity of 82% and a specificity of 80.8% using a single-reference pair. Notably, the system achieved high clinical safety by detecting 96.3% of severe overt hypothyroidism cases (free T4 &lt;0.5 ng/dL). Conclusions: This AI-assisted system demonstrates the feasibility of a sensitive, noninvasive longitudinal monitoring tool for hypothyroidism, robustly identifying a high proportion of hypothyroid cases—particularly severe ones—to prioritize at-risk individuals for confirmatory blood tests. Given the predominantly female iatrogenic-withdrawal cohort, prospective sex-balanced validation in primary hypothyroidism is warranted. <img src="https://jmir-production.s3.us-east-2.amazonaws.com/thumbs/a987102a2705d0b3e60602b091360ef2" />

<h4>Objective</h4>To describe the epidemiological characteristics, public health response, and evidence of international spread of a large measles outbreak that contributed to the loss of Canada's measles elimination status.<h4>Design</h4>Descriptive analysis of the cohort of measles outbreak cases reported to Ontario's public health surveillance system during the outbreak period: epidemiological week of 28 October 2024 to 6 October 2025.<h4>Setting</h4>Ontario, Canada.<h4>Participants</h4>Individuals meeting the outbreak case definition for confirmed or probable measles infection during the outbreak period.<h4>Exposure</h4>Exposure to measles virus associated with the Ontario outbreak and related transmission chains.<h4>Main outcome measures</h4>Demographic characteristics, vaccination status, admission to hospital, complications, pregnancy associated outcomes, mortality, and evidence of international spread based on measles virus genotyping and sequencing. Cases were identified using an outbreak-specific case definition incorporating epidemiological, clinical, laboratory, and viral sequencing criteria. Phylogenetic analysis of measles virus sequences was used to assess evidence of onwards spread to other countries.<h4>Results</h4>The outbreak consisted of 2377 cases (2064 confirmed and 313 probable) over 49 weeks and contributed to the loss of Canada's measles elimination status. Most cases (2146/2377, 90.3%) occurred among members of historically under-vaccinated communities, with limited spillover into the broader population. Among the 2377 cases, approximately three quarters of cases (n=1736, 73.0%) occurred in individuals aged 19 years or younger, 2121 (89.2%) were unvaccinated, and 97 (4.1%) had unknown vaccination status. The outbreak was associated with 166 (7%) admissions to hospital, 13 (<1%) admissions to intensive care unit, 51 (2.1%) infections during pregnancy, 10 (<1%) congenital measles infections, one (<1%) case of encephalitis, and one (<1%) infant death. Sequencing identified a novel N450 distinct sequence identifier that was subsequently detected in countries including the United States of America, Mexico, and the Netherlands. The outbreak strain was designated a World Health Organization named strain (MVs/Ontario.CAN/47.24).<h4>Conclusions</h4>These findings highlight the risk of measles re-establishment in countries that have attained elimination and underscore the importance of culturally appropriate community engagement to build trust in immunisation and prevent future outbreaks. This outbreak shows the fragility of measles elimination in the presence of long standing immunity gaps within interconnected communities across the Americas and the population-level protection that limited broader community transmission.

### [Left Bundle-Branch Area Pacing vs Right Ventricular Pacing for Atrioventricular Block and Preserved Left Ventricular Function: The LEAP-Block Randomized Clinical Trial.](https://doi.org/10.1001/jama.2026.18146)

- Source: `jama_abstracts` · published `2026-10-28` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `pending` · status: `failed`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `pmid:42949742`

<h4>Importance</h4>For patients with atrioventricular block requiring high-burden ventricular pacing, the optimal pacing strategy to reduce heart failure (HF) and all-cause mortality remains uncertain. Robust comparative evidence on the clinical outcomes of left bundle-branch area pacing (LBBAP) vs right ventricular pacing (RVP) in this population is needed.<h4>Objective</h4>To compare 24-month clinical outcomes of LBBAP vs RVP in patients with atrioventricular block and preserved left ventricular ejection fraction (LVEF; ≥50%).<h4>Design, setting, and participants</h4>The LEAP-Block study was a prospective, multicenter, randomized, controlled, superiority trial conducted across 14 Chinese hospitals from January 2021 to August 2025, with last follow-up on June 3, 2025. Of 551 patients with LVEF 50% or greater requiring ventricular pacing who underwent eligibility assessment, 499 with anticipated ventricular pacing greater than 40% were randomized.<h4>Interventions</h4>Patients were randomized in a 1:1 ratio to LBBAP (n = 249) or RVP (n = 250).<h4>Main outcomes and measures</h4>The primary end point was a composite of all-cause death, HF events, or upgrade to biventricular pacing at 24 months. Secondary end points included pacing-induced cardiomyopathy and device-related complications.<h4>Results</h4>The primary analysis included 482 patients (LBBAP, n = 242; RVP, n = 240; mean age, 67 years; 46% female). Acute procedure success was comparable between the 2 groups (LBBAP, 237/242 [97.9%] vs RVP, 239/240 [99.6%]; P = .22), with left bundle-branch capture confirmed in 188 of 236 patients (79.7%). LBBAP significantly reduced the primary end point vs RVP (2.4% vs 12.5%, respectively; absolute risk difference, -10.1% [95% CI, -16.8% to -3.4%]; P = .003). Among secondary outcomes, the LBBAP group had fewer HF events (3.4% vs 11.3%; -7.9% [95% CI, -13.0% to -2.8%]). No significant differences were observed in all-cause death, biventricular pacing upgrade, device-related complications, or pacing-induced cardiomyopathy. The 24-month Kaplan-Meier estimated cumulative incidence of the primary end point was 5.0% for LBBAP vs 13.2% for RVP (hazard ratio, 0.37 [95% CI, 0.19 to 0.73]; P = .004). LBBAP superiority was consistent across all prespecified subgroups.<h4>Conclusions and relevance</h4>Among patients with atrioventricular block and preserved LVEF requiring high-burden ventricular pacing, LBBAP was superior to RVP in reducing the risk of the composite end point of all-cause death, HF events, or upgrade to biventricular pacing at 24 months.<h4>Trial registration</h4>ClinicalTrials.gov Identifier: NCT04730921.

### [Secure Patient Messaging in Ophthalmology: Retrospective Natural Language Processing Study](https://medinform.jmir.org/2026/1/e96026)

- Source: `jmir_medinform` · published `2026-10-28` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `pending` · status: `failed`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `url:https://medinform.jmir.org/2026/1/e96026`

Background: The rapid rise of secure patient messaging has transformed ophthalmic communication, with text volumes now exceeding prepandemic levels across health care systems. Our study uses a natural language processing (NLP) model to explore patterns in over 30,000 secure messages and identify common ocular complaints and differences by patient demographics. Objective: The objective of this study is to characterize the clinical and administrative concerns communicated through secure ophthalmology messaging and to assess differences in message content across patient sociodemographic groups. Methods: We conducted a cross-sectional study of deidentified, patient-initiated secure messages sent between June 2014 and July 2024. Patients with ophthalmic conditions who initiated secure electronic health record (EHR) portal messages were included. Of the 52,388 extracted message threads, 30,390 (58%) patient medical advice request (PMAR) messages routed solely to the Department of Ophthalmology from 4817 unique patients were assessed after the exclusion of questionnaires, courtesy messages, clinician responses, and messages routed to other health care departments. Of these unique patients, 2673 (55.5%) were female, 2740 (56.9%) were aged 50 years or older, 2346 (48.7%) were White, 4392 (91.2%) were non-Hispanic, and 2774 (57.6%) were married. NLP and large language model (LLM)–assisted topic classification were used to categorize message content. The distribution of message topics and frequency of clinical concerns were assessed and stratified by age, sex, race, ethnicity, and marital status. Differences in message frequency by demographic subgroup were assessed using 2-proportion z-tests. Results: Following preprocessing and topic modeling, 14,284 (47%) of the 30,390 messages could be predefined in relation to a topic cluster and were included in the final analysis. Nearly half of all 14,284 predefined messages addressed administrative issues, including 4166 (29.2%) scheduling-related inquiries, 1352 (9.5%) pharmacy and medication refill requests, and 581 (4.1%) insurance document issues. Among clinical concerns, of the total 14,284 predefined messages, 2975 (20.8%) messages involved vision disturbances, 1241 (8.7%) involved glaucoma-related symptoms, 1074 (7.5%) involved tumor-related questions, and 1064 (7.4%) involved surgery-relevant concerns. Message content differed significantly by demographic characteristics. Non-White patients more frequently raised issues related to pharmacy refills, insurance, glaucoma, and disability documentation, whereas White patients more often reported surgical concerns. Older patients more frequently messaged about glaucoma, surgery, and tumor-related issues, while female patients more often reported complications and swelling or infection. Conclusions: Secure patient messages frequently include clinically relevant symptoms with potential triage implications and demonstrate demographic differences in care-seeking behavior. Systematic analysis of message content may support safer triage, improved workflow efficiency, and more equitable delivery of ophthalmic care.

### [5-year outcomes of chemotherapy de-escalation in HER2-positive early breast cancer and exploratory circulating tumor DNA analyses from the randomized phase 2 PHERGain trial](https://www.nature.com/articles/s41591-026-04723-w)

- Source: `nature_medicine` · published `2026-10-29` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `pending` · status: `failed`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `url:https://www.nature.com/articles/s41591-026-04723-w`

PHERGain was a randomized, non-comparative phase 2 trial evaluating a positron 18F-fluorodeoxyglucose-positron emission tomography (18F-FDG-PET)-guided, pathological complete response (pCR)-adapted strategy to omit chemotherapy in HER2-positive early breast cancer. Female patients with HER2-positive stages I–IIIA operable breast cancer (tumor size ≥ 1.5 cm) were randomized to receive chemotherapy plus trastuzumab and pertuzumab (HP; group A) or HP ± endocrine therapy (group B). In group B, treatment was adapted according to 18F-FDG-PET response after two cycles and pCR, with chemotherapy administered to nonresponders or patients without pCR. We previously reported that this de-escalation approach revealed a 3-year invasive disease-free survival (iDFS) of 94.3% (95% confidence interval (CI): 90.9 to 96.8) in group B. Here we report the prespecified secondary endpoint, 5-year iDFS and exploratory circulating tumor DNA (ctDNA) findings. After a median follow-up of 5.6 years, the 5-year iDFS was 89.5% (95% CI: 85.7 to 93.4) in group B (n = 267) and 92.4% (95% CI: 86.7 to 98.5) among 18F-FDG-PET responders achieving pCR without chemotherapy (n = 86), with one distant recurrence. The latter estimate describes a long-term outcome in a post-randomization subgroup defined by 18F-FDG-PET response and pCR and should be interpreted as descriptive rather than as causal effects of omitting chemotherapy. Detectable ctDNA at baseline or during treatment was associated with inferior iDFS, whereas ctDNA clearance correlated with higher pCR rates and improved long-term outcomes. These findings continue to support the clinical validity of an 18F-FDG-PET-guided, pCR-adapted approach and suggest a potential role for ctDNA in refining treatment individualization. ClinicalTrials.gov identifier: NCT03161353 . A 5-year secondary analysis of the phase 2 PHERGain trial supports the clinical utility of a PET-guided adaptive strategy for treatment de-escalation in HER2-positive early breast cancer and provides circulating tumor DNA insights that could inform further treatment approaches.

### [A clinical multimodal vision language foundation model with fine-grain explainability](https://www.nature.com/articles/s41591-026-04655-5)

- Source: `nature_medicine` · published `2026-10-29` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `pending` · status: `failed`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `url:https://www.nature.com/articles/s41591-026-04655-5`

Despite their considerable promise, many current medical foundation models remain modality specific and lack transparent reasoning, limiting clinical adoption. To address this gap, we introduce Explainable Vision Language Foundation Model for Medicine (EVLF-FM), a multimodal vision language foundation model that combines broad diagnostic capabilities with fine-grained explainability. The development and testing of EVLF-FM encompassed more than 1.3 million total samples from 18 global datasets across 10 imaging modalities related to six clinical specialties: dermatology, hepatology, ophthalmology, pathology, pulmonology and radiology. External validation employed 13,443 independent test samples from 17 additional datasets across 10 imaging modalities. EVLF-FM supports disease diagnosis, visual question answering and pixel-level visual grounding with reasoning capabilities. In internal validation for disease diagnostics, EVLF-FM achieved the highest average accuracy, 0.895, outperforming leading generalist and specialist models. In medical visual grounding, EVLF-FM also achieved reasonable performance across nine modalities with average accuracy at 0.5 intersection over union (Acc@0.5) of 0.842. External testing further confirmed consistent zero-shot and few-shot performance, with competitive F1 scores despite a smaller model size. A hybrid training strategy combining supervised and visual reinforcement fine-tuning enables EVLF-FM to generate clinically interpretable explanations aligned with visual evidence. Human evaluation of these reasoning outputs using the S.C.O.R.E. framework yielded a high overall mean score of 4.25/5, supporting their clinical interpretability. Current limitations include the need for additional validation across diverse healthcare settings. Prospective clinical studies are required to evaluate the safety, effectiveness and clinical utility of EVLF-FM before routine clinical deployment. A multimodal vision language foundation model, EVLF-FM, integrates extensive clinical data and advanced training techniques, achieving superior diagnostic accuracy and explainability across diverse imaging modalities, enhancing clinical decision-making through robust reasoning and interpretability.

### [A Multistage Fine-Tuning and Empathy Alignment Approach for an Emotional Support Dialogue System: Development and Evaluation Study](https://www.jmir.org/article/view/jmir_v28i1e95603)

- Source: `jmir` · published `2026-10-29` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `pending` · status: `failed`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `url:https://www.jmir.org/article/view/jmir_v28i1e95603`

Background: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly being used for text-based emotional support. However, general-purpose LLMs may not consistently recognize emotional cues, adapt supportive strategies to contexts, or generate responses aligned with psychologically informed communication. The limited availability of large-scale, structured Chinese counseling data further constrains the development of models for context-sensitive emotional support. Objective: We developed and evaluated YuanChat, a family of specialized LLMs for context-sensitive emotional support; assessed whether E-URIAL improved response quality; and compared YuanChat with base LLMs. Methods: We constructed 2 datasets from anonymized real-world counseling sessions: ECD-3 (104,897 counselor response turns) for fine-tuning and (9413 manually annotated counselor responses). Annotations covered emotional support conversation stages (exploration, comfort, and action), counseling techniques (clarification, support, and challenge), and empathic effects. We developed 2 YuanChat variants using GLM-4-9B-Chat and Huozi 3.5 as backbone models using parameter-efficient fine-tuning with low-rank adaptation (LoRA). We then developed E-URIAL, an inference-time framework combining predicted emotion and support strategy labels with retrieved labeled exemplars. Given the dialogue history through the help seeker’s latest utterance, each model generated a counselor-style response. We compared YuanChat with GPT-4, ERNIE 4.0, CharacterGLM, GLM-4-9B-Chat, and Huozi 3.5. The evaluation comprised (1) automatic evaluation on in-domain and external datasets, assessing empathy identification and empathic expression; (2) evaluation of empathy-related situational reasoning using an expert-developed multiple-choice benchmark; (3) evaluation by clinical experts (n=8) of empathic attunement, support strategy appropriateness, perceived helpfulness, and safety and professional boundary adherence; and (4) a complementary evaluation by volunteer master’s students in clinical and counseling psychology (n=50) of response fluency, empathy, and perceived helpfulness. Results: Fine-tuning increased YuanChat-Huozi3.5’s empathic expression score from 0.57 to 0.68; empathy identification score increased from 0.54 to 0.59. In expert evaluation, YuanChat-Huozi3.5 received a higher empathic attunement score than the base Huozi3.5 model (1.279 vs 0.327; mean difference 0.952, 95% CI 0.746‐1.152). Using professional counselor responses as the reference, the mean differences for YuanChat-Huozi3.5 were 0.148, 0.079, 0.039, and 0.002 in empathic attunement, supportive appropriateness, perceived helpfulness, and safety and professional boundary adherence, respectively. No statistically significant differences were detected between YuanChat-Huozi3.5 and the counselor references (all Holm-adjusted ≥.29). Higher scores were observed when E-URIAL was applied. When E-URIAL was applied with 3 retrieved exemplars, YuanChat-Huozi3.5 achieved an empathy identification score of 0.69 versus 0.59 without E-URIAL, an empathic expression score of 0.79 versus 0.68, and a perceived helpfulness score of 0.74 versus 0.61. Conclusions: The findings indicate that fine-tuning on real-world counseling data and annotating models with structured emotional support labels improved context-sensitive empathic responding in LLMs. Without updating model parameters, E-URIAL further improved empathy identification and empathic expression across multiple models. Together, these methods provide a scalable, theory-informed approach to enhancing the emotional support capabilities of LLMs. <img src="https://jmir-production.s3.us-east-2.amazonaws.com/thumbs/b19b96599a801d9536ac422b4a8f6dba" />

### [Abbott grows cardiac portfolio with Amplatzer approval for valve leaks](https://www.medtechdive.com/news/abbott-grows-cardiac-portfolio-with-amplatzer-approval-for-valve-leaks/873163/)

- Source: `medtech_dive_primary` · published `2026-10-29` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `pending` · status: `failed`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `url:https://www.medtechdive.com/news/abbott-grows-cardiac-portfolio-with-amplatzer-approval-for-valve-leaks/873163/`

Abbott said Wednesday it received Food and Drug Administration approval for a transcatheter device designed to close a small gap that can develop between the heart tissue and a new valve after replacement surgery, causing blood to leak. Such paravalvular leaks occur in 7% to 17% of mitral valve replacements and 5% to 10% of aortic valve replacements, the company said. The Amplatzer Paraclose PVL Occluder is the first minimally invasive device approved in the U.S. to close paravalvular leaks around surgical replacement heart valves and offers an alternative to additional open-heart surgery for addressing the problem.

### [Assessing Digital Determinants of Health and Their Impact as Part of Strategic Health Planning in Catalonia: Modified Delphi Study](https://www.jmir.org/article/view/jmir_v28i1e93840)

- Source: `jmir` · published `2026-10-29` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `pending` · status: `failed`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `url:https://www.jmir.org/article/view/jmir_v28i1e93840`

&lt;strong&gt;Background:&lt;/strong&gt; Digital health technologies are transforming health care by improving access, efficiency, and quality of care. However, recent studies highlight that digitalization can unintentionally reinforce or deepen existing social and health inequalities, as not all population groups benefit equally from digital progress. The concept of digital determinants of health (DDoH) has emerged to better capture how digital factors shape health opportunities and outcomes. The systematic incorporation of DDoH and other health determinants shaped by digitalization into strategic planning remains limited. As a result, health systems may lack structured approaches to identify them and anticipate their equity implications. &lt;strong&gt;Objective:&lt;/strong&gt; This study aimed to identify which health determinants are considered most important in the Catalan context and to qualitatively explore their inclusion in existing regional strategies and policy frameworks in Catalonia. &lt;strong&gt;Methods:&lt;/strong&gt; We conducted a modified Delphi study with experts from the Catalan health and digital ecosystem. The list of determinants was derived from a scoping review, which identified 127 determinants that emerged or changed with digitalization. Experts living and working in Catalonia, with experience in digital health, public health, health care delivery, social or DDoH, or health equity, were recruited using snowball sampling. Participants rated each determinant for importance using a 5-point Likert scale across 2 rounds, followed by a final online consensus meeting. For each determinant, the median (IQR) and the proportion of experts rating it as 4 or 5 were calculated, and items were classified into high, moderate, or low relevance according to predefined consensus criteria. &lt;strong&gt;Results:&lt;/strong&gt; A total of 43 experts participated in the first Delphi round, and 31 completed the second round. Of the 127 assessed determinants, 20 (15.7%) reached high relevance for the Catalan context, including 15 digital, 4 social, and 1 political determinant. Overall, the most important determinants focused on enabling the effective and equitable use of digital health, particularly access, skills, system-level digital capacity and governance, inclusive design, and the responsible use of AI. Comparison with the original global Delphi study showed substantial overlap, but also revealed context-specific priorities. &lt;strong&gt;Conclusions:&lt;/strong&gt; This study demonstrates the value of systematically contextualizing DDoH and other health determinants shaped by digitalization to inform strategic health planning in digitally mature public health systems. The findings show that international digital health agendas require regional reprioritization to reflect local infrastructure, governance arrangements and equity challenges. The results suggest that, beyond technological capacity, governance and equity considerations should become central to digital health planning. The proposed approach offers a replicable framework to support more anticipatory, coordinated and equity-oriented digital health strategies. <img src="https://jmir-production.s3.us-east-2.amazonaws.com/thumbs/d5bfe88d2d050befcde0860fdb4618ed" />

After spinal cord injury, clinicians raise blood pressure to protect tissue without directly monitoring the effect on the cord, exposing patients to harm. We designed a closed-loop controller that adjusts drug infusion in response to ultrasonography-based measurements of spinal cord blood flow, stabilizing perfusion at the injury site and preserving neurological function in animals.

### [Data Stream and Hospital-Level Variation in Intensive Care Unit Vital-Sign Record Density Across Medical Information Mart for Intensive Care IV and eICU Collaborative Research Database: Retrospective Data Quality Study](https://medinform.jmir.org/2026/1/e99638)

- Source: `jmir_medinform` · published `2026-10-29` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `pending` · status: `failed`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `url:https://medinform.jmir.org/2026/1/e99638`

<strong>Background:</strong> Electronic health record–derived process measures such as vital-sign record density are used for quality monitoring, risk prediction, and cross-system benchmarking, but whether they retain consistent meaning across sites and data streams is rarely tested. <strong>Objective:</strong> This study aimed to determine whether vital-sign record density varies primarily by data stream, database, contributing site, or care unit, and what follows for comparative use. <strong>Methods:</strong> We retrospectively analyzed the Medical Information Mart for Intensive Care IV (MIMIC-IV) version 3.1 and the eICU Collaborative Research Database (eICU-CRD) version 2.0. Record density and timing metrics were computed for the first 24 intensive care unit hours from nurse-charted streams and, in eICU-CRD, from the monitor stream. Heart rate was primary; oxygen saturation and respiratory rate were replication variables. Variance was decomposed by database, hospital, and unit using η<sup>2</sup> with hospital-clustered bootstrap inference and mixed-effects sensitivity analyses. A hospital-level plausibility floor of 12 nurse-charted heart rate observations per 24 hours was applied for the principal analysis; unrestricted and alternative-floor analyses are reported as sensitivities. <strong>Results:</strong> A pooled-item MIMIC-IV heart rate count included alarm-limit documentation events, so label-based pooling mixes the two. In 117,572 eICU-CRD stays present in both streams, nurse- and monitor-derived counts were almost unrelated (Spearman ρ=0.024), with bottom-decile overlap at chance. Between-database η<sup>2</sup> for record count was 0.960 against the eICU monitor stream and 0.026 against the nurse stream. Eight of 68 eICU-CRD hospitals fell below the plausibility floor and were excluded from principal estimates, a restriction introduced during review. Among the remaining 60 hospitals (88,560 stays), hospital η<sup>2</sup> for record count was 0.354 (95% CI 0.259-0.450) against unit-within-hospital η<sup>2</sup> of 0.043; for the interval-structure metrics, the hospital and unit components were not separable. For record count, the hospital component fell from 0.441 unrestricted to 0.321 at an hourly floor. A negative-binomial mixed model gave latent-scale hospital and unit variance-partition coefficients of 0.348 (95% CI 0.252-0.457) and 0.112 (0.078-0.163), equivalent to 0.318 and 0.110 observed. A pooled bottom-decile rule agreed with hospital-specific rules on 52.5% (4726/9006) of flagged stays, with 6 hospitals supplying half the pooled decile. The hospital component for record count replicated at 0.322 for oxygen saturation and 0.333 for respiratory rate. <strong>Conclusions:</strong> Within a multisite nurse-charted stream, vital-sign record volume varied more across contributing sites than across care units within hospitals; for the interval-structure metrics, the site and unit components were not separable at this cohort size. Apparent cross-database differences were reproduced by source-table choice, and site-level variation remained after excluding the most evident contribution-completeness outliers. Comparative use therefore requires specifying the generating stream, enforcing the analytic window, and explicitly modeling site-level variation; the residual site component should not be interpreted as documentation practice alone.

### [Digital Health Applications for Mental Disorders in Germany Among Users and Nonusers on Actual and Intended Use, Self-Reported Completion, and Reasons for Discontinuation: Cross-Sectional Survey](https://www.jmir.org/article/view/jmir_v28i1e99464)

- Source: `jmir` · published `2026-10-29` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `pending` · status: `failed`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `url:https://www.jmir.org/article/view/jmir_v28i1e99464`

&lt;strong&gt;Background:&lt;/strong&gt; Digital health interventions, including mobile health apps, increasingly complement outpatient mental health care. In Germany, certified digital health applications (DiGA [Digitale Gesundheitsanwendungen]) can be prescribed and reimbursed in the statutory health care system. However, real-world evidence on patient-reported use of DiGA for mental disorders remains limited. &lt;strong&gt;Objective:&lt;/strong&gt; This study aimed to analyze usage patterns and experiences among DiGA users and expectations among nonusers. Among users, we examined duration of active use, self-reported completion, and experienced reasons for discontinuation; among nonusers, whether DiGA use would be an option and the perceived relevance of hypothetical reasons for discontinuation. &lt;strong&gt;Methods:&lt;/strong&gt; We conducted an anonymous cross-sectional mixed-mode survey among adults insured by Techniker Krankenkasse, Germany’s largest statutory insurer. Random samples of 2000 individuals per group were invited in April 2024, and data collection ended in September 2024. Users received an activation code for a DiGA authorized for mental disorders. Nonusers had a documented diagnosis for which a DiGA was available but had no prior use. Analyses were descriptive, using available cases, frequencies, and medians with IQRs for ordinal Likert-type items. Actual and intended use were treated as distinct. Exploratory subgroup analyses were conducted. &lt;strong&gt;Results:&lt;/strong&gt; Overall, 1413 participants were included in the analysis (769 of 2000 invited users; 644 of 2000 invited nonusers), with response rates of 38.5% (95% CI 36.3%-40.6%) and 32.2% (95% CI 30.2%-34.3%), respectively. A total of 840 of 1249 (67.3%; 95% CI 64.6%-69.8%) participants were female. Nonusers were older and more often lived in rural areas. Among users, 487 of 687 (70.9%; 95% CI 67.4%-74.2%) reported active use for at least 5 weeks; 409 of 700 (58.4%; 95% CI 54.7%-62.0%) reported completion of their DiGA program. The proportion of reported completions increased from 111 of 250 (44.4%; 95% CI 38.4%-50.6%) among those aged 18-40 years to 85 of 112 (75.9%; 95% CI 67.2%-82.9%) among those aged 61 years and older. The most frequently endorsed reasons for discontinuation among users reporting noncompletion were lack of motivation (151/272, 55.5%; 95% CI 49.6%-61.3%) and insufficient personalization (114/255, 44.7%; 95% CI 38.7%-50.8%). Among nonusers, the most frequently endorsed hypothetical reasons for discontinuation were mismatch with their condition (431/540, 79.8%; 95% CI 76.2%-83.0%) and technical difficulties (432/545, 79.3%; 95% CI 75.7%-82.5%). &lt;strong&gt;Conclusions:&lt;/strong&gt; This study provides patient-reported real-world evidence on certified and reimbursable digital mental health interventions. Many users reported weeks of active use, but noncompletion remained common and was more frequent among younger users. Lack of motivation as a frequently reported reason for discontinuation suggests that supporting continued engagement may be important during DiGA use. Beyond Germany, these findings may support implementation of digital mental health interventions in other health care systems. &lt;strong&gt;Trial Registration:&lt;/strong&gt; <img src="https://jmir-production.s3.us-east-2.amazonaws.com/thumbs/cac96a12ace8b0b31c965ae5b890d044" />

### [Identifying Burnout in Pediatric Clinicians Using Wearable Biometric Data: Development and Feasibility Study](https://www.jmir.org/article/view/jmir_v28i1e104511)

- Source: `jmir` · published `2026-10-29` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `pending` · status: `failed`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `url:https://www.jmir.org/article/view/jmir_v28i1e104511`

Background: Addressing the burnout epidemic among health care providers remains one of the foremost challenges of modern health care systems. Past researchers have turned to passively gathered biometric data from wearable devices to identify key variables that predict burnout. However, this has resulted in limited success. Objective: This pilot and feasibility study aimed to test the feasibility and accuracy of developing an Extreme Gradient Boosting machine learning model to classify health care providers as being at higher or lower risk of burnout based on their biometric data. Methods: We conducted a 3-month pilot and feasibility study where health care providers self-reported burnout every 6 weeks and wore a Garmin Venu 3 smartwatch to collect continuous biometric data. Participants were recruited from 3 pediatric urgent care sites and an inpatient hospitalist division of a pediatric health care system. We had a final sample of 41 participants who met our inclusion criteria requiring completion of burnout measurements at 2 of the 3 time points and having worn the watch for at least 70% of the study period. Participants were asked to wear the Garmin watch continuously over the course of the study. Burnout self-report surveys were delivered via email. Our main outcome was burnout as measured using the Maslach Burnout Inventory–Human Services Survey. The measure consists of 3 subscales that together constitute burnout: emotional exhaustion, depersonalization, and reduced personal efficacy. Results: First, we found evidence of feasibility for wearable watches to be used in situ for health care providers to gather data passively for the purposes of classifying burnout. A total of 82% (51/62) of participants met our criteria of wearing the watch 70% of the time, with 66% (41/62) having corresponding burnout scores to be included in analyses. Second, our machine learning model achieved strong overall performance in identifying risk of burnout, with strong sensitivity and specificity, demonstrating that biometric data collected using wearable devices can accurately predict burnout. Conclusions: Our evidence of feasibility offers promising results for a passive method of data collection to be used for classifying burnout risk in other health care settings. Furthermore, the strong performance of our machine learning model provides a foundational step toward improving an organization’s ability to respond and mitigate burnout through thoughtful intervention strategies. <img src="https://jmir-production.s3.us-east-2.amazonaws.com/thumbs/1252a11aef07721bf300898bbfe85e30" />

### [Insights into early-onset colorectal cancer from a single-nucleus RNA-seq atlas of colorectal cancer across the age continuum](https://www.nature.com/articles/s41591-026-04721-y)

- Source: `nature_medicine` · published `2026-10-29` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `pending` · status: `failed`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `url:https://www.nature.com/articles/s41591-026-04721-y`

The incidence of early-onset colorectal cancer (EO-CRC; diagnosed before age 50 years) is increasing worldwide. Although current data indicate that EO-CRC may be biologically distinct from average-onset CRC (AO-CRC; diagnosed at age ≥50 years), molecular differences remain poorly defined. To address this knowledge gap, we generated a single-nucleus RNA sequencing (snRNA-seq) atlas from 189 primary CRC tumors (54 EO-CRC, 135 AO-CRC) spanning eight decades of life, with at least 20 tumors in most age decades. Our analyses stratified tumors by microsatellite status and accounted for tumor sidedness, stage, patient sex and neoadjuvant treatment history, modeling age continuously or categorically (EO-CRC versus AO-CRC). Among microsatellite-stable (MSS) tumors, those from younger patients were characterized by increased stromal and decreased epithelial proportions, which we confirmed using multispectral immunofluorescence and machine learning in an external cohort of 712 MSS CRC tumors (18 EO-CRC, 694 AO-CRC). Tumor epithelial cells from young patients exhibited increased expression of long noncoding RNAs and FN1, with a trend toward increased heterogeneity in copy number alterations. MSS left-sided EO-CRC tumors had more cell–cell interactions, with epithelial-to-mesenchymal transition-related signaling pathways (FN1/GDF/ACTIVIN) showing increased, cell type-specific interactions. Our large high-resolution snRNA-seq atlas uncovers age-associated features of CRC and provides a resource for the community to understand EO-CRC biology. A high-resolution snRNA-seq atlas of 189 colorectal tumors from patients aged 20–90+ years uncovers age-associated features of colorectal cancer.

### [Irritable bowel syndrome and related disorders of gut-brain interaction.](https://doi.org/10.1016/s0140-6736(26)01500-x)

- Source: `lancet_abstracts` · published `2026-10-29` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `pending` · status: `failed`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `pmid:42956794`

Irritable bowel syndrome, chronic constipation, and functional diarrhoea are disorders of gut-brain interaction, the symptoms of which include abnormal stool consistency or frequency accompanied by varying degrees of abdominal pain, discomfort, bloating, or distension. These conditions affect 20% of the global population with a relapsing and remitting course. Risk factors include female sex, younger age, a history of common mental disorders, and acute enteric infection. The pathophysiology of irritable bowel syndrome, chronic constipation, and functional diarrhoea remain incompletely understood, but are thought to be secondary to aberrant interactions throughout the gut-brain axis, resulting in disordered gut motility and visceral hypersensitivity. These conditions are diagnosed with a combination of clinical history, absence of alarm symptoms-such as weight loss or rectal bleeding-and targeted investigations. An empathetic management approach improves quality of life and symptoms, reduces health-care use, and enhances treatment adherence. Mainstays of treatment include education, dietary and lifestyle advice, modifying fibre intake, and drugs directed towards predominant symptoms. For severe symptoms, other treatments include specific medical devices, biofeedback, or psychological therapies. However, the effectiveness of most treatments is modest and there is an ongoing need to expand available treatments for bowel disorders, with several novel drugs currently in development.

### [Optimal closed-loop feedback control of blood flow after spinal cord injury with norepinephrine](https://www.nature.com/articles/s41551-026-01777-6)

- Source: `nature_biomedical_engineering` · published `2026-10-29` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `pending` · status: `failed`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `url:https://www.nature.com/articles/s41551-026-01777-6`

After spinal cord injury, clinical management aims to support tissue perfusion, but current methods rely on the indirect control of arterial blood pressure without direct measurement of the local blood flow. This limits the ability to tailor drug delivery to the injured tissue. Here, we show that spinal cord blood flow can be regulated using a closed-loop controller that adjusts vasopressor infusion on the basis of real-time ultrasound flow measurement. We characterize the dynamic relationship between drug infusion, arterial pressure and spinal cord blood flow, and we use these data to design a feedback controller that targets a predefined flow level. We validate the system in simulations, implement the controller in real time in rodents and translate it to pigs. We show that the controller reduced blood flow deviations and variability compared with constant dosing and supported functional recovery in a rodent survival model. These results show a closed-loop spinal cord perfusion control strategy that leverages clinically available measurements and pharmacological interventions, supporting its translation to clinical settings for the management of acute spinal cord injury. A closed-loop control strategy regulates spinal cord blood flow by adjusting norepinephrine infusion using real-time flow measurements, enabling direct flow targeting after acute injury.

### [Performance of 0/1 h versus 0/3 h pathways in patients with suspected myocardial infarction: a systematic review and individual participant data meta-analysis of randomised controlled trials.](https://doi.org/10.1016/s0140-6736(26)01767-8)

- Source: `lancet_abstracts` · published `2026-10-29` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `pending` · status: `failed`
- Event: `updated` · identity: `pmid:42956796`

<h4>Background</h4>In patients with suspected myocardial infarction, the 0/1 h pathway (blood draws at 0 h and 1 h) is recommended in current guidelines for early risk stratification. However, individual trials used different safety outcomes, and efficacy outcomes were inconclusive or not assessed. We conducted a systematic review and individual participant data meta-analysis of all randomised controlled trials to synthesise the totality of the evidence, assess heterogeneity across trials, and evaluate clinically relevant outcomes using harmonised definitions across trials.<h4>Methods</h4>In this systematic review and individual participant data meta-analysis, we searched PubMed, Cochrane Central Register of Controlled Trials, Scopus, and the International Clinical Trials Registry Platform from inception to July 6, 2026, without language restrictions, for randomised trials comparing the safety and efficacy of 0/1 h versus 0/3 h pathways (blood draws at 0 h and 3 h, ie, standard care). Individual participant data were obtained and outcome definitions were harmonised. The primary analysis used a two-stage random-effects meta-analysis. After receipt of the individual participant data but before data harmonisation or analysis, the primary safety outcome was refined to a composite of all-cause death or new type 1 myocardial infarction within 30 days, from the originally specified composite of all-cause death or any new myocardial infarction. The primary efficacy outcomes were emergency department length of stay and direct discharge from the emergency department. This meta-analysis is registered with PROSPERO (CRD420261383452).<h4>Findings</h4>Our systematic review identified five randomised trials, all of which provided individual participant data. Among these, the definition of the primary safety outcome varied, and in four trials emergency department length of stay was not reported for the whole analysis population. We included 110 933 patient presentations (55 489 managed with the 0/1 h pathway and 55 444 managed with the 0/3 h pathway). Within 30 days, all-cause death or new type 1 myocardial infarction occurred in 446 patient presentations (0·8%) with the 0/1 h pathway versus 562 patient presentations (1·0%) with the 0/3 h pathway (random-effects pooled odds ratio [OR] 0·83 [95% CI 0·45-1·53]; I<sup>2</sup>=62·9%; τ<sup>2</sup>=0·143). Emergency department length of stay was 314 min (IQR 211-481) in the 0/1 h pathway group versus 308 min (209-453) in the 0/3 h pathway group (random-effects pooled geometric mean ratio 1·03 [95% CI 0·85-1·25]; I<sup>2</sup>=99·2%; τ<sup>2</sup>=0·024). Direct discharge from the emergency department occurred in 37 190 patient presentations (67·0%) with the 0/1 h pathway versus 36 485 patient presentations (65·8%) with the 0/3 h pathway (random-effects pooled OR 1·31 [95% CI 0·79-2·16]; I<sup>2</sup>=97·8%; τ<sup>2</sup>=0·156).<h4>Interpretation</h4>Across the available randomised trials, short-term adverse event rates were low with both pathways, but the pooled safety estimate remained imprecise. On average, earlier cardiac troponin retesting did not reduce emergency department length of stay or clearly increase direct discharge, and substantial between-study heterogeneity indicates that treatment effects might not translate uniformly across health-care systems. For hospitals considering transitioning from a 0/3 h to a 0/1 h pathway, implementation decisions should take broader diagnostic and organisational considerations into account.<h4>Funding</h4>Swiss National Science Foundation, Swiss Heart Foundation, University Hospital Basel, University of Basel, and Foundation for Cardiovascular Research Basel.

### [The Current Roles of AI in Public Health Practice: Scoping Review](https://www.jmir.org/article/view/jmir_v28i1e108250)

- Source: `jmir` · published `2026-10-29` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `pending` · status: `failed`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `url:https://www.jmir.org/article/view/jmir_v28i1e108250`

Background: AI is a broad field encompassing various techniques, which include algorithms that learn from data to perform automated tasks without explicit human programming. Public health aims to protect and improve the health of communities and populations at the local, regional, national, and global levels, but it typically has limited resources. While AI may lead to improved efficiency or effectiveness of public health interventions, the adoption of AI interventions should be informed by evidence of effective use in practice. Objective: This review aims to summarize how AI-assisted interventions are currently used by public health professionals. Methods: We conducted a scoping review to identify the current uses of AI in public health. We searched MEDLINE (Ovid), Embase (Ovid), Web of Science (Core Collection), and Scopus for relevant reports published between January 1, 2020, and July 24, 2025. Two reviewers independently screened reports. Eligible reports were primary studies (experimental or observational), commentaries, and editorials that discussed how AI tools have been used in practice in public health contexts relevant to high-income countries, excluding outbreaks. Data were extracted by one reviewer and checked by a second. Results: We screened 5400 records and 155 full texts, including 8 reports. These reports discussed a variety of uses of AI tools across public health domains: 3 reports used chatbots in different settings, 1 report used machine learning to forecast health service demand, 2 reports used AI for symptom checking, and 2 reports used AI for evidence synthesis. While assessing the effectiveness of AI tools for public health was beyond the scope of this review, and most reports did not robustly evaluate effectiveness, the evidence for the effectiveness of AI tools was nonetheless mixed. One report described a chatbot that users found helpful, while another found a specific chatbot to be ineffective, highlighting its potential to undermine trust in the organization that developed it. Another report highlighted both benefits and drawbacks of a large language model used to communicate with socially isolated individuals. The main finding of this review was that even with a comprehensive search across multiple databases, there were few reports of how AI tools are being used in practice in public health contexts. Conclusions: The lack of evidence from public health practice, excluding outbreaks, makes it difficult to know which uses of AI, if any, would be useful to incorporate into practice in high-income countries. In the absence of evidence, new interventions, including those using AI, should be evaluated to ensure they are effective (and cost-effective). These evaluations should be widely and accessibly disseminated to create an evidence base that other public health teams could use to inform their own decisions around AI tools. <img src="https://jmir-production.s3.us-east-2.amazonaws.com/thumbs/53e7e0a29c12c5328178278d01684286" />

### [A single-step injectable Janus-adhesive hydrogel for endoscopic gastrointestinal fistula repair](https://www.nature.com/articles/s41551-026-01820-6)

- Source: `nature_biomedical_engineering` · published `2026-10-30` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `pending` · status: `failed`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `url:https://www.nature.com/articles/s41551-026-01820-6`

Gastrointestinal fistulas (GFs) represent one of the main causes of morbidity and mortality. While endoscopic techniques provide a minimally invasive approach to GF repair, offering advantages over conventional surgical approaches, the integration of endoscopic delivery with hydrogel sealants in user-friendly and easy processes approaches remains a great challenge. Here we develop a thermoresponsive positively charged poly(N-isopropylacrylamide) (PNIPAM)/Ga3+ (PGaIII) hydrogel sealant, formed by slug-inspired biomimetic self-assembly. The PGaIII integrated injectability and single-step-formed Janus-type adhesion to tissues, simultaneously featuring excellent biocompatibility, water/acid resistance, active shrinkage and antibacterial properties, enabling its effective ready-to-use biological sealant for endoscopic repair of GFs and prevention of postoperative adhesions. To validate the efficacy of PGaIII in treating GFs, we demonstrate its effectiveness in repairing GFs and its convenience in non-invasive endoscopic surgery in living Sprague–Dawley rat and Bama miniature pig models. The proposed PGaIII holds potential for the repair of fistulas in other organs in vivo, thereby promoting the establishment of safer and more effective clinical treatment options for related diseases. A thermoresponsive hydrogel sealant, prepared in a user-friendly and single-step process, and featuring Janus-type adhesion, is injected endoscopically and repairs gastrointestinal fistulas in rat and minipig models.

### [Acceptability of Wearable Digital Devices for Remote Continuous Mobility Monitoring in People With Parkinson Disease and Carers: Qualitative Study](https://www.jmir.org/article/view/jmir_v28i1e98987)

- Source: `jmir` · published `2026-10-30` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `pending` · status: `failed`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `url:https://www.jmir.org/article/view/jmir_v28i1e98987`

Background: Parkinson disease (PD) impairs mobility and motor function, making accurate mobility assessment important for effective management and treatment planning. Traditional clinical assessments often rely on periodic clinical evaluations, which may not capture day-to-day symptom fluctuations. Wearable devices offer a promising alternative with continuous, objective tracking of mobility (eg, gait). However, although these tools provide valuable insights for patients and clinicians, their successful implementation depends on their acceptability to users. Objective: This study aimed to explore the acceptability of wearable devices for remote continuous mobility monitoring in PD from the perspectives of people with PD and informal carers. Methods: This international qualitative study used purposive sampling to recruit a diverse sample of 72 people with PD and 20 informal carers from the United Kingdom, Ireland, Belgium, Germany, Spain, and the United States. Participants were recruited between June 2023 and February 2026 through multiple community-based routes. One-to-one semistructured interviews, conducted online, via telephone, or in person, explored views on wearable devices for digital mobility assessment. Interview data were analyzed using Braun and Clarke’s 6-phase reflexive thematic analysis. Results: Most people with PD were generally open to wearable monitoring when it could capture motor fluctuations between “on” and “off” periods that were difficult to convey during clinical appointments, reduce reliance on memory, provide objective evidence to support treatment and medication discussions and self-advocacy, and track changes in mobility over time, which some felt could be reassuring. Acceptability depended on devices having a clear purpose, requiring minimal effort, and being easy to use. Tremor, reduced dexterity, and “off” periods could make fastening straps, navigating menus, and charging devices difficult. Carers valued wearable monitoring for the reassurance and safety it offered, particularly fall detection, and for support with both clinical and everyday decision-making but emphasized that devices must operate passively and integrate into daily caregiving without adding burden. Conclusions: Wearable mobility monitoring was generally acceptable to people with PD and carers when it offered clear clinical value, fit easily into everyday life, and provided reassurance that remote monitoring would complement rather than replace clinical care. As PD mobility was experienced as a shared concern within the care relationship, the value of monitoring depended on how well it supported the wider care dyad rather than the person with PD alone. These findings suggest that successful implementation will require co-design with people with PD and carers, alongside thoughtful clinical integration, to ensure that wearable technologies are both acceptable and effective in practice. <img src="https://jmir-production.s3.us-east-2.amazonaws.com/thumbs/5f8ad29d0b7b76a8e1908835a35fc4b5" />

### [Accuracy and impact of an AI safety system for missed specialist referrals in primary care](https://www.nature.com/articles/s41746-026-03444-1)

- Source: `npj_digital_medicine` · published `2026-10-30` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `pending` · status: `failed`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `url:https://www.nature.com/articles/s41746-026-03444-1`

Missed specialist referrals in primary care pose substantial health and medico-legal risks, yet remain poorly understood. We developed an AI-supported safety system (MR-AI) to identify missed referrals from consultation notes, evaluated in a quasi-experimental study across seven primary care clinics in Singapore. MR-AI scanned ~25,000 notes weekly for referral intent without a corresponding order, achieving an estimated sevenfold increase in weekly missed referral detection (4.7 percentage point level increase; 95% CI 0.024–0.069; p &lt; 0.001). Of 428 cases flagged, 225 were clinician-verified, alongside 31 patient-reported cases, yielding 256 total detections. MR-AI demonstrated 99.9% specificity and 48.8% sensitivity. In a blinded one-week comparison against seven physician reviewers, MR-AI achieved a PPV of 61.8% versus a human confirmation rate of 51.8% across 83 flagged cases, while requiring approximately 41 fewer projected staff-hours. AI-enabled surveillance substantially assisted in missed referral detection, though sustainable improvement will require complementary preventive workflow changes and future cost-effectiveness assessment.

### [Admission Mechanisms for Scalable Federated Health Data Collaboration: Design and Proof-of-Concept Study](https://medinform.jmir.org/2026/1/e89685)

- Source: `jmir_medinform` · published `2026-10-30` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `pending` · status: `failed`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `url:https://medinform.jmir.org/2026/1/e89685`

Background: Many digital health and AI initiatives show promising results in pilot settings but do not progress to sustainable services. Pilots can operate within a predefined collaboration, whereas operational services must accommodate changes in participating organizations, available resources, and analytical capabilities while preserving established governance and technical arrangements. Objective: This study examines the pilot-to-service transition as a problem of governed federation evolution and evaluates whether Open Health can admit new forms of participation while preserving bounded capabilities, organizational sovereignty, and auditable decision evidence. Methods: Open Health applies federated computing (FC) as a governance model separated from the analytical runtime. A governed federation context is established before execution, participants receive holder-bound capabilities, and an admission gateway produces explicit ALLOW or DENY decisions before requested operations proceed. The proof of concept uses the Flower federated learning framework and the Pathology Modified National Institute of Standards and Technology (PathMNIST) dataset from the MedMNIST collection with an initial federation comprising hospitals A and B. Mode 1A introduces hospital C as a sponsored contributor permitted to submit locally computed training updates but not invoke the resulting federated model for inference. Mode 1B examines bounded AI-agent participation, where restricted results may be transformed through policy-authorized operations into derivatives whose consumption is governed independently from the source. Evaluation considers admission conformance, signed decision evidence, analytical effects of the additional contribution, and admission overhead. Results: The demonstrator admitted valid member operations and hospital C’s sponsored contribution (mode 1A) while denying model consumption outside hospital C’s capability, scope violations, holder-binding mismatch, and federation-envelope mismatch. Mode 1B extended the same bounded-governance pattern to AI-agent participation, admitting bounded inference and governed transformation but denying unauthorized source consumption and privileged governance access. Under matched training conditions, cancer-related macro recall increased from 50.2% in the A+B control to 67.4% in A+B+C (+17.2 percentage points), while noncancer macro recall changed from 85.5% to 86.3% (+0.8 percentage points). Recall increased from 19.5% to 39.9% for cancer-associated stroma and from 80.9% to 94.9% for colorectal adenocarcinoma epithelium. Median (IQR) full admission latency was 1.210 (1.141-1.333) ms for ALLOW, 1.195 (1.136-1.273) ms for capability-scope DENY, and 0.884 (0.837-0.921) ms for holder-binding DENY. Each evaluated request generated signed decision evidence recording the decision, reason, participant and request bindings, governance context, and related model run. Conclusions: Open Health demonstrates how a federated health-data collaboration can evolve through differentiated participation while preserving membership boundaries and bounded capability relations. The principal contribution is an executable governance boundary preserving capability relations and producing independently verifiable decision evidence separately from the federated-learning runtime. The analytical experiment showed that governed admission of an additional contributor produced a measurable effect on the intended cancer-related classes while preserving noncancer performance. The proof of concept establishes governance behavior under controlled conditions; clinical effectiveness and production-scale performance require further evaluation.

### [AnySleep: a channel-agnostic deep learning system for high-resolution sleep staging in multi-center cohorts](https://www.nature.com/articles/s41746-026-03406-7)

- Source: `npj_digital_medicine` · published `2026-10-30` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `pending` · status: `failed`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `url:https://www.nature.com/articles/s41746-026-03406-7`

Sleep is essential for health, yet studying its dynamics requires manual sleep staging, a labor-intensive step in research and clinical care. Across centers, polysomnography (PSG) recordings are traditionally scored in 30-s epochs for pragmatic, not physiological, reasons and vary in electrode count, montage, and subject characteristics. These constraints challenge harmonized multi-center studies and the discovery of robust biomarkers on shorter timescales. We present AnySleep, a deep neural network that scores sleep from any electroencephalography (EEG) or electrooculography (EOG) data at adjustable temporal resolutions. We trained and validated the model on over 20,000 overnight recordings (&gt;200,000 h of EEG and EOG) from 28 datasets across multiple clinics to promote robust generalization across sites. The model attains state-of-the-art performance and surpasses or equals established baselines at 30-s epochs. Performance improves with more channels, yet remains strong when EOG is absent, or only EOG or single EEG derivations (frontal, central, or occipital) are available. On sub-30-s timescales, the model captures short wake intrusions consistent with arousals and improves the prediction of pathophysiological conditions (obstructive sleep apnea, narcolepsy type 1, insomnia) over 30-s scoring. We make the model publicly available to facilitate large-scale studies with heterogeneous electrode setups and accelerate biomarker discovery in sleep.
